) is equal to

(2.k)

\prod_{\mu^{\prime},\lambda,\gamma}\mu^{\prime}(A_{\varrho^{\prime}_{\mu^{\prime}}\lambda,\gamma})^{\mu^{\prime}\nu_{i}},

which lies in
\prod_{w^{\prime}|v_{i}}T(L^{\prime}_{w^{\prime}})
. This immediately yields the equation

(2.l)

t_{\varrho^{\prime},\sigma^{\prime}}=t_{\varrho,\sigma},\quad\varrho^{\prime},\sigma^{\prime}\in{\rm Gal}(L^{\prime}/F),

since
t^{-1}_{\varrho,\sigma}t_{\varrho^{\prime},\sigma^{\prime}}
on one hand lies in
T(L^{\prime})
and on the other hand lies in

\prod_{w^{\prime}|v_{1}}T(L^{\prime}_{w^{\prime}})\prod_{w^{\prime}|v_{2}}T(L^{\prime}_{w^{\prime}}).

It is also clear that the local homomorphisms outside
v_{1}
and
v_{2}
are equivalent. We also want to show that, for example,

\textstyle{\mathscr{D}^{L^{\prime}_{w_{1}^{\prime}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathscr{T}^{\prime}_{\nu_{1}^{\prime},\nu_{2}^{\prime}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathscr{D}^{L_{v_{1}^{\prime}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathscr{T}_{\nu_{1},\nu_{2}}}

is commutative. On the right is the isomorphism resulting from (
2.e
). The commutativity on the kernel of
\mathscr{D}^{L^{\prime}_{w^{\prime}_{1}}}
is clear. What else needs to be considered is the projection of (
2.k
) onto
T(L^{\prime}_{w^{\prime}_{1}})
, namely

\prod_{\gamma\in H_{1}}A^{\nu_{1}}_{\varrho^{\prime},\gamma}(1)=G^{\nu_{1}}_{\varrho^{\prime}}.

Therefore, it suffices to show the following equation:

A^{\prime}_{\varrho^{\prime},\sigma^{\prime}}(1)=A^{l_{1}}_{\varrho^{\prime},\sigma^{\prime}}(1)G^{-1}_{\varrho^{\prime}}\varrho^{\prime}(G_{\sigma^{\prime}})^{-1}G_{\varrho^{\prime}\sigma^{\prime}},\quad\varrho^{\prime},\sigma^{\prime}\in G^{\prime}_{1},

where both expressions are contained in the group
L^{\prime\times}_{w^{\prime}_{1}}
.

We
†
†
margin:

133

have already seen that

\displaystyle A^{\prime}_{\varrho^{\prime},\sigma^{\prime}}(1)

\displaystyle=\prod_{\gamma\in H}A^{-1}_{\varrho^{\prime},\gamma}(1)A_{\varrho^{\prime},\sigma^{\prime}\gamma}(1)

\displaystyle=\prod_{\gamma}A^{-1}_{\varrho^{\prime},\gamma}(1)A_{\varrho^{\prime},\gamma\nu}(1),

where
\nu
is the representative of
\sigma^{\prime}
in the right representative system. We have also seen that the product on the right hand side is equal to
\prod_{\gamma}A_{\varrho^{\prime}\gamma,\nu}(1)
. We are interested in this product as an element of
L^{\prime}_{w_{1}^{\prime}}
, so we can project it onto the corresponding coordinate and obtain

\prod_{\gamma\in H_{1}}A_{\varrho^{\prime}\gamma,\nu}(1).

This product, in turn, is equal to

\prod_{\gamma\in H_{1}}A^{-1}_{\varrho^{\prime},\sigma^{\prime}}(1)A_{\varrho^{\prime},\sigma^{\prime}\gamma}(1)=A^{l_{1}}_{\varrho^{\prime},\sigma^{\prime}}(1)G^{-1}_{\varrho^{\prime}}\varrho^{\prime}(G^{-1}_{\sigma^{\prime}})G_{\varrho^{\prime}\sigma^{\prime}}.

The construction of
\mathscr{T}_{\nu_{1},\nu_{2}}
is obviously functorial in
T
,
\nu_{1}
, and
\nu_{2}
. Namely, if we have a homomorphism
\phi:T\to T^{\prime}
defined over
F
and
\nu^{\prime}_{i}=\phi(\nu_{i})
for
i=1,2
, then we immediately obtain a homomorphism
\phi
from
\mathscr{T}_{\nu_{1},\nu_{2}}
to
\mathscr{T}_{\nu^{\prime}_{1},\nu^{\prime}_{2}}
.

We formulate some of the results obtained above in a theorem.

Theorem 2.2
.

Let
T
be a torus defined over
F
that splits over the Galois extension
L
of
F
. Let
v_{1}
and
v_{2}
be two places of
F
, and let
v^{\prime}_{1}
and
v^{\prime}_{2}
be chosen extensions to
L
. Let two cocharacters
\nu_{1}
and
\nu_{2}
in
X_{*}(T)
be given, satisfying conditions (
2
) and (
2
). Then the above construction defines a gerbe
\mathscr{T}=\mathscr{T}_{\nu_{1},\nu_{2}}
over
F
with kernel
T
, equipped with homomorphisms

\displaystyle\zeta_{v}:{\rm Gal}_{F_{v}}\longrightarrow\mathscr{T},\quad v\neq v_{1},v_{2},

\displaystyle\zeta_{v_{i}}:\mathscr{D}^{L_{v^{\prime}_{i}}}\longrightarrow\mathscr{T},\quad i=1,2,

such that
\zeta_{v_{i}}
on the kernel is given by
x\mapsto x^{\nu_{i}}
. This gerbe is uniquely determined up to an isomorphism that transforms the local homomorphisms into equivalent ones. If
L^{\prime}\supset L
is a larger Galois extension to which the places
v^{\prime}_{i}
are extended to
w^{\prime}_{i}
, then the above construction yields an isomorphism

\mathscr{T}^{\prime}_{\nu_{1}^{\prime},\nu_{2}^{\prime}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\longrightarrow}}\mathscr{T}_{\nu_{1},\nu_{2}},

where
†
†
margin:

134

\nu^{\prime}_{i}=[L^{\prime}_{w^{\prime}_{i}}:L_{v^{\prime}_{i}}]\cdot\nu_{i}\quad\text{for}\quad i=1,2,

such that the following two diagrams commute up to equivalence:

\textstyle{\mathscr{T}^{\prime}_{\nu_{1}^{\prime},\nu_{2}^{\prime}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\sim}
\textstyle{{\rm Gal}_{F_{v}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\zeta^{\prime}_{v}}
\scriptstyle{\zeta_{v}}
\textstyle{\text{for }v\neq v_{1},v_{2},}
\textstyle{\mathscr{T}_{\nu_{1},\nu_{2}}}

\textstyle{\mathscr{D}^{L^{\prime}_{w_{i}^{\prime}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathscr{T}^{\prime}_{\nu_{1}^{\prime},\nu_{2}^{\prime}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\sim}
\textstyle{\mathscr{D}^{L_{v_{i}^{\prime}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathscr{T}_{\nu_{1},\nu_{2}}.}

The construction is functorial in
T
,
\nu_{1}
, and
\nu_{2}
.

One way to achieve the above situation is for
\nu_{1}
and
\nu_{2}
to arise from averaging the same cocharacter. Let
T
be a torus over
F
, and let
\mu
be an element of
X_{*}(T)
. We set

\nu_{i}=(-1)^{i+1}\sum_{\sigma\in\text{Gal}(L_{v^{\prime}_{i}}/F_{v_{i}})}\sigma\mu.

We will introduce a canonical homomorphism
\tau_{\mu}
from the corresponding gerbe
\mathscr{T}_{\nu_{1},\nu_{2}}
to the neutral gerbe
\mathscr{G}_{T}
.

Before doing so, we define in a very simple way a homomorphism
\xi_{\mu}
from
\mathscr{D}^{L_{v^{\prime}}}
to
\mathscr{G}_{T}
. The field
L
splits
T
, and
v^{\prime}
is a prime of
L
dividing a prime
v
of
F
. Let

\nu=\sum_{\sigma\in\text{Gal}(L_{v^{\prime}}/F_{v})}\sigma\mu.

The homomorphism
\xi_{\mu}
is defined as follows:

\xi_{\mu}(z)=z^{\nu},\quad z\in L_{v^{\prime}}^{\times},

\xi_{\mu}(d_{\varrho})=\prod_{\sigma\in\text{Gal}(L_{v^{\prime}}/F_{v})}d_{\varrho,\sigma}^{\varrho\sigma\mu}\rtimes\varrho,

where
d_{\varrho,\sigma}
is the cocycle defining
\mathscr{D}^{L_{v^{\prime}}}
, and
d_{\varrho}=d^{L_{v^{\prime}}}_{\varrho}
. It is easy to verify that
\xi_{\mu}
is indeed a homomorphism.

The homomorphism
\tau_{\mu}
to be introduced has the following properties:

(i)

\tau_{\mu}\circ\zeta_{v_{1}}
is equivalent to
\xi_{\mu}
;

(ii)

\tau_{\mu}\circ\zeta_{v_{2}}
is equivalent to
\xi_{-\mu}
;

(iii)

\tau_{\mu}\circ\zeta_{v}
is the canonical neutralization of
\mathscr{G}_{T}
.

The homomorphism
\tau_{\mu}
is defined by

\tau_{\mu}:t_{\varrho}\longmapsto s_{\varrho}\rtimes\varrho,

where
†
†
margin:

135

s_{\varrho}\in T(L)
and

s_{\varrho}\varrho(s_{\sigma})s_{\varrho\sigma}^{-1}=t_{\varrho,\sigma}.

Furthermore, there exists
f\in T(\mathbb{A}_{L})
such that

fe_{\varrho}s_{\varrho}\varrho(f^{-1})=e^{\prime}_{\varrho}

satisfies the following properties:

(i)

the projection of
e^{\prime}_{\varrho}
onto
L_{v^{\prime}}
is
1
if
v^{\prime}
divides neither
v_{1}
nor
v_{2}
;

(ii)

the projection of
e^{\prime}_{\varrho}
onto
T(L_{v^{\prime}_{i}})
is

\prod_{\sigma\in\text{Gal}(L_{v^{\prime}_{i}}/F_{v_{i}})}A_{\varrho,\sigma}^{(-1)^{i+1}\varrho\sigma\mu}(i).

Let
\{B_{\varrho}\}
be the cocycle appearing in (
2.c
). We set

F=\prod_{\varrho\in\text{Gal}(L/F)}B_{\varrho}^{-\varrho\mu},

E_{\varrho}(i)=\prod_{\tau\in\mathfrak{S}_{i}}\prod_{\sigma\in\text{Gal}(L_{v^{\prime}_{i}}/F_{v_{i}})}A_{\varrho\tau,\sigma}^{(-1)^{i+1}\varrho\tau\sigma\mu}(i),

so

E_{\varrho}(i)\in\prod_{v^{\prime}|v_{i}}T(L_{v^{\prime}}).

A straightforward calculation yields the equation

(2.m)

E_{\varrho}=E_{\varrho}(1)E_{\varrho}(2)F\varrho(F^{-1}).

Indeed, for
\tau\in\mathfrak{S}_{i}
and
\sigma\in\text{Gal}(L_{v^{\prime}_{i}}/F_{v_{i}})
, we have

A_{\varrho,\tau\sigma}(i)=A_{\varrho\tau,\sigma}(i)A_{\varrho,\tau}(i),

so
C_{\varrho}(i)
is the product of

\prod_{\sigma\in\text{Gal}(L/F)}A_{\varrho,\sigma}^{(-1)^{i}\varrho\sigma\mu}(i)

and

\prod_{\tau\in\mathfrak{S}_{i}}\prod_{\sigma\in\text{Gal}(L_{v^{\prime}_{i}}/F_{v_{i}})}A_{\varrho\tau,\sigma}^{(-1)^{i+1}\varrho\tau\sigma\mu}=E_{\varrho}(i).

Consequently,

\displaystyle E_{\varrho}

\displaystyle=\left\{\prod_{\sigma}A_{\varrho,\sigma}^{-\varrho\sigma\mu}(1)A_{\varrho,\sigma}^{\varrho\sigma\mu}(2)\right\}\left\{B_{\varrho}^{\eta}E_{\varrho}(1)E_{\varrho}(2)\right\}

\displaystyle=\left\{\prod_{\sigma}\big(B_{\varrho}\varrho(B_{\sigma})B_{\varrho\sigma}^{-1}\big)^{-\varrho\sigma\mu}\right\}\left\{B_{\varrho}^{\eta}E_{\varrho}(1)E_{\varrho}(2)\right\}

\displaystyle=F^{-1}\varrho(F)E_{\varrho}(1)E_{\varrho}(2).

The
†
†
margin:

136

elements
E_{\varrho}(i)
have already been lifted to
T(\mathbb{A}_{L})
. We lift
F
to an element
f
and obtain

s_{\varrho}e_{\varrho}f\varrho(f^{-1})=E_{\varrho}(1)E_{\varrho}(2)

with
s_{\varrho}\in T(L)
. Thus,

e^{\prime}_{\varrho}=E_{\varrho}(1)E_{\varrho}(2),

so
e^{\prime}_{\varrho}
clearly possesses the desired properties.

It must be checked once again whether
s_{\varrho}
is independent of the choices made in its construction, up to a coboundary. These choices are enumerated as (i) to (v) below. The possibility mentioned under (v) requires special attention.

(i) If we take a lift
e^{\prime\prime}_{\varrho}=r_{\varrho}e_{\varrho}
with
r_{\varrho}\in T(L)
, then
t_{\varrho,\sigma}
is modified to
t^{\prime\prime}_{\varrho,\sigma}=r_{\varrho}^{-1}\varrho(r_{\sigma}^{-1})r_{\varrho\sigma}t_{\varrho,\sigma}
, and the isomorphism between the two resulting gerbes is defined by

t^{\prime\prime}_{\varrho}\longmapsto r_{\varrho}^{-1}t_{\varrho}.

Since
s_{\varrho}
is replaced by
r_{\varrho}^{-1}s_{\varrho}
,
t^{\prime\prime}_{\varrho}
maps to
r_{\varrho}^{-1}s_{\varrho}\rtimes\varrho
in the neutral gerbe
\mathscr{G}_{T}
, which is also the image of
r_{\varrho}^{-1}t_{\varrho}
.

(ii) If we replace
B_{\varrho}
by
B_{\varrho}G\varrho(G^{-1})
, then
F
is multiplied by

\prod_{\varrho}G^{-\varrho\mu}\varrho(G)^{\varrho\mu}.

If we lift
G
to
g
, we can choose

f^{\prime}=f\prod_{\varrho}g^{-\varrho\mu}\varrho(g)^{\varrho\mu}

instead of
f
, and

f^{\prime}\varrho(f^{\prime})^{-1}=f\varrho(f^{-1})g^{-\eta}\varrho(g^{\eta}).

We have already seen that
e_{\varrho}
is replaced by
e_{\varrho}g^{\eta}\varrho(g^{-\eta})
. Thus,
\{s_{\varrho}\}
remains unchanged.

(iii) A similar calculation shows compatibility under changes of the representatives
\omega_{\tau}
for
\tau\in\mathfrak{S}_{i}
. If we change the system of representatives
\mathfrak{S}_{i}
itself, then
B_{\varrho}
is multiplied by an element of
\prod_{v^{\prime}|v_{i}}L_{v^{\prime}}^{\times}
. Consequently, neither
t_{\varrho,\sigma}
nor
s_{\varrho}
is changed.

(iv) A change in the embedding
W_{L_{v^{\prime}_{1}}/F_{v_{1}}}\to W_{L/F}
does not alter the cocycles and thus does not change
\{s_{\varrho}\}
. A change in
\omega_{\varrho}
for
\varrho\in\text{Gal}(L_{v^{\prime}_{1}}/F_{v_{1}})
multiplies
B_{\varrho}
by an element of
\prod_{v^{\prime}|v_{1}}T(L_{v^{\prime}})
and causes no change in
\{s_{\varrho}\}
.

(v) If
†
†
margin:

137

we change
v^{\prime}_{1}
and replace it with
v^{\prime\prime}_{1}
,

|\lambda x|_{v^{\prime\prime}_{1}}=|x|_{v^{\prime}_{1}},

then

\sum_{\sigma\in\text{Gal}(L_{v^{\prime\prime}_{1}}/F_{v_{1}})}\sigma\mu=\lambda\sum_{\sigma\in\text{Gal}(L_{v^{\prime}_{1}}/F_{v_{1}})}\sigma\lambda^{-1}\mu,

and in general, this will only equal
\lambda\nu_{1}
if
\lambda
can be chosen such that
\lambda^{-1}\mu=\mu
. Therefore, we assume this condition. However, even in this case,
\tau_{\mu}
is not independent of
v^{\prime\prime}_{1}
. Indeed, let
\widetilde{A}_{\varrho,\sigma}
be liftings of
A_{\varrho,\sigma}
, and let
C_{\varrho,\sigma,\tau}
be the Teichmüller cocycle defined by

\varrho(\widetilde{A}_{\sigma,\tau})\widetilde{A}_{\varrho\sigma,\tau}^{-1}\widetilde{A}_{\varrho,\sigma\tau}\widetilde{A}_{\varrho,\sigma}^{-1}=C_{\varrho,\sigma,\tau}.

Then

r_{\varrho}=\prod_{\sigma\in\text{Gal}(L/\mathbb{Q})}C_{\varrho,\sigma,\lambda}^{-\varrho\sigma\mu}

is a 1-cocycle of
\text{Gal}(L/\mathbb{Q})
with values in
T(L)
, and
\tau_{\mu}
is replaced by
\tau^{\prime}_{\mu}:t_{\varrho}\mapsto r_{\varrho}\tau_{\mu}(t_{\varrho})
when
v^{\prime}_{1}
is replaced by
v^{\prime\prime}_{1}
.

Indeed,
E_{\varrho}
is multiplied by the expression (
2.e
), which is equal to

\varrho(Y)Y^{-1}\varrho(Z_{1})Z_{\varrho}^{-1}.

On the other hand,
F
is multiplied by

U=\prod_{\sigma\in\text{Gal}(L/F)}D_{\sigma}^{-\sigma\mu}.

By definition,

U=\prod_{\sigma}\lambda(A_{\lambda^{-1},\sigma\lambda})^{\sigma\mu}A_{\sigma,\lambda}^{\sigma\mu}A_{\lambda,\lambda^{-1}}^{-\sigma\mu}

and

Y=\prod_{\tau\in\mathfrak{S}_{1}}A_{\lambda,\lambda^{-1}}^{-\tau\nu_{1}}\prod_{\tau\in\mathfrak{S}_{1}}\lambda(A_{\lambda^{-1},\tau})^{\tau\nu_{1}}.

Consequently,

UY^{-1}=\prod_{\sigma}\left(\lambda(A_{\lambda^{-1},\sigma\lambda})^{\sigma\mu}A_{\sigma,\lambda}^{\sigma\mu}\right)\prod_{\tau}\lambda(A_{\lambda^{-1},\tau})^{\tau\nu_{1}}.

Due to the cocycle property, the first product on the right hand side is equal to

\prod_{\sigma}\lambda(A_{\lambda^{-1}\sigma,\lambda}A_{\lambda^{-1},\sigma})^{\sigma\mu}=\prod_{\sigma}A_{\sigma,\lambda}^{\sigma\mu}\prod_{\sigma}\lambda(A_{\lambda^{-1},\sigma})^{\sigma\mu}.

If
†
†
margin:

138

\sigma
lies in
\tau\text{Gal}(L_{v^{\prime}_{1}}/F_{v_{1}})
, then

\lambda(A_{\lambda^{-1},\sigma})\equiv\lambda(A_{\lambda^{-1},\tau})\quad\text{mod}\quad\tau(L_{v^{\prime}_{1}}).

We can therefore lift
UY^{-1}
to the product of
\prod_{\sigma}\widetilde{A}_{\sigma,\lambda}^{\sigma\mu}

and an element of

\prod_{v^{\prime}|v_{1}}T(L_{v^{\prime}}).
Furthermore,

\prod_{\sigma}\widetilde{A}_{\sigma,\lambda}^{\sigma\mu}\prod_{\sigma}\varrho(\widetilde{A}_{\sigma,\lambda})^{-\varrho\sigma\mu}=\prod_{\sigma}C_{\varrho,\sigma,\lambda}^{-\varrho\sigma\mu}=r_{\varrho},

since

\prod_{\sigma}\widetilde{A}_{\varrho,\sigma\lambda}^{\varrho\sigma\mu}\widetilde{A}_{\varrho,\sigma}^{-\varrho\sigma\mu}=1.

Thus,
s^{\prime}_{\varrho}=r_{\varrho}s_{\varrho}
.

Finally, we want to show that
\tau_{\mu}
is independent of
L
. Let
L^{\prime}\supset L
be a Galois extension of
F
. We reuse the earlier formulas. To avoid using the same symbol with two meanings, we write

B^{l}_{\varrho^{\prime}}=B_{\varrho}B_{\varrho^{\prime}}(1)B_{\varrho^{\prime}}^{-1}(2)G\varrho(G)^{-1}.

From the earlier calculations, it follows that
E_{\varrho^{\prime}}
is the product of
E_{\varrho}
and two other factors. One of these factors is irrelevant for
s_{\varrho^{\prime}}
because it lies in

\prod_{v^{\prime}|v_{1}}T(L_{v^{\prime}})\prod_{v^{\prime}|v_{2}}T(L_{v^{\prime}}).

The other factor is

G^{\eta}\varrho(G)^{-\eta}\prod_{i=1}^{2}\left\{\prod_{\mu^{\prime}}B_{\mu^{\prime}}(i)^{\mu^{\prime}\nu_{i}}\prod_{\mu^{\prime}}\varrho(B_{\mu^{\prime}}(i))^{-\varrho\mu^{\prime}\nu_{i}}\right\}.

On the other hand,

F^{\prime}=\prod_{\varrho^{\prime}\in\text{Gal}(L^{\prime}/F)}B_{\varrho^{\prime}}^{-\varrho^{\prime}\mu}.

We show that

F^{\prime}F^{-1}G^{\eta}\prod_{i=1}^{2}\prod_{\mu^{\prime}}B_{\mu^{\prime}}(i)^{\mu^{\prime}\nu_{i}}\equiv V\quad\text{modulo}\quad\prod_{v^{\prime}|v_{1},v_{2}}T(L_{v^{\prime}}),

where
V
is not only invariant but actually the image of an element in
T(\mathbb{A}_{F})
. Thus,
s_{\varrho^{\prime}}=s_{\varrho}
.
Indeed, we show that

W=\left(\prod_{\gamma\in H}B_{\gamma}^{-1}\right)G

lies in
C_{L}
, and

V=\prod_{\varrho\in\text{Gal}(L/\mathbb{Q})}\varrho(W)^{\varrho\mu},

which suffices, since
\mathbb A_{L}\to C_{L}
is surjective.

Let
†
†
margin:

139

\varrho^{\prime}\in\text{Gal}(L^{\prime}/F)
, and let
\mu_{i}^{\prime}
be its representative modulo
HG_{i}^{\prime}
. It must be shown that

(2.n)

{\varrho^{\prime}}^{-1}\left(\prod_{\gamma\in H}B^{-1}_{\varrho^{\prime}\gamma}B_{\varrho}GB_{\mu^{\prime}_{1}}(1)B^{-1}_{\mu^{\prime}_{2}}(2)\right)

is congruent to

(2.o)

\prod_{\gamma\in H}B^{-1}_{\gamma}G,

with equality if
\varrho^{\prime}\in H
.

First, we have

\displaystyle{\varrho^{\prime}}^{-1}\left(\prod_{\gamma\in H}B^{-1}_{\varrho^{\prime}\gamma}\right)

\displaystyle=\prod_{\gamma}B_{\sigma^{\prime}}~\cdot~\prod_{\gamma}B^{-1}_{\gamma}\cdot A^{-1}_{\sigma^{\prime},\varrho^{\prime}\gamma}(1)A_{\sigma^{\prime},\varrho^{\prime}\gamma}(2)

\displaystyle=B^{l}_{\sigma^{\prime}}\prod_{\gamma}\big(A^{-1}_{\sigma^{\prime},\varrho^{\prime}\gamma}(1)A_{\sigma^{\prime},\varrho^{\prime}\gamma}(2)\big)\prod_{\gamma}B^{-1}_{\gamma},

where
\sigma^{\prime}={\varrho^{\prime}}^{-1}
. Thus, (
2.n
) is equal to

(2.p)

B_{\sigma^{\prime}}(1)B^{-1}_{\sigma^{\prime}}(2)\prod_{\gamma}\big(A^{-1}_{\sigma^{\prime},\varrho^{\prime}\gamma}(1)A_{\sigma^{\prime},\varrho^{\prime}\gamma}(2)\big)\sigma^{\prime}\big(B_{\mu_{1}^{\prime}}(1)B^{-1}_{\mu_{2}^{\prime}}(2)\big)\sigma(B_{\varrho})B_{\sigma}

times (
2.o
).

Second, we have

\sigma^{\prime}\left(B_{\mu_{i}^{\prime}}(i)\right)B_{\sigma^{\prime}}(i)=B_{\sigma^{\prime}\mu_{i}^{\prime}}(i)A^{l}_{\sigma^{\prime},\mu_{i}^{\prime}}(i)A^{-1}_{\sigma,\mu_{i}}(i),

and

B_{\sigma^{\prime}\mu_{i}^{\prime}}(i)=\prod_{\gamma\in H}A_{\sigma^{\prime}\mu_{i}^{\prime},\gamma}(i),

since
\sigma^{\prime}\mu_{i}^{\prime}\in HG_{i}^{\prime}
.

If
\varrho^{\prime}\in H
, then
B_{\varrho}=B_{\sigma}=1
,
\mu_{i}^{\prime}=1
, and

\prod_{\gamma}A_{\sigma^{\prime},\varrho^{\prime}\gamma}(i)=\prod_{\gamma}A_{\sigma^{\prime},\gamma}(i),

which yields the desired equality.

In general, if
\varrho^{\prime}\in\mu_{i}^{\prime}H\nu_{i}
with
\nu_{i}
from the right representative system of
H_{i}
in
G_{i}^{\prime}
, then

\prod_{\gamma}A_{\sigma^{\prime},\varrho^{\prime}\gamma}(i)=\prod_{\gamma}A_{\sigma^{\prime},\mu_{i}^{\prime}\gamma\nu_{i}}(i)

and

A_{\sigma^{\prime},\mu_{i}^{\prime}\gamma\nu_{i}}(i)A^{-1}_{\sigma^{\prime},\mu_{i}^{\prime}\gamma}(i)=\sigma^{\prime}\left(A_{\mu_{i}^{\prime}\gamma,\nu_{i}}(i)^{-1}\right)A_{\sigma^{\prime}\mu_{i}^{\prime}\gamma,\nu_{i}}(i)\in\sigma^{\prime}\mu_{i}^{\prime}\gamma(L^{\prime}_{w_{i}^{\prime}}).

Furthermore,

A^{-1}_{\sigma^{\prime},\mu_{i}^{\prime}\gamma}(i)A_{\sigma^{\prime}\mu_{i}^{\prime},\gamma}(i)A_{\sigma^{\prime},\mu_{i}^{\prime}}(i)=\sigma^{\prime}\left(A_{\mu_{i}^{\prime},\gamma}(i)\right)=1.

Thus,
†
†
margin:

140

(
2.p
) is equal to

\sigma(B_{\varrho})B_{\sigma}A^{-1}_{\sigma,\mu_{1}}(1)A_{\sigma,\mu_{2}}(2).

But

A_{\sigma,\mu_{i}}(i)\equiv A_{\sigma,\varrho}(i)\pmod{L^{\prime}_{w_{i}^{\prime}}},

and

\sigma(B_{\varrho})B_{\sigma}A^{-1}_{\sigma,\varrho}(1)A_{\sigma,\varrho}(2)=1,

since
\varrho\sigma=1
.

We now formulate the main result in a theorem.

Theorem 2.3
.

In the situation of Theorem
2.2
, let
\mu
be an element of
X_{*}(T)
that yields the cocharacters
\nu_{i}
by averaging:

\nu_{i}=(-1)^{i+1}\sum_{\sigma\in{\rm Gal}(L_{v_{i}^{\prime}}/F_{v_{i}})}\sigma\mu.

Then the above construction provides a homomorphism
\tau_{\mu}
from the gerbe
\mathscr{T}_{\nu_{1},\nu_{2}}
to the neutral gerbe
\mathscr{G}_{T}
, such that
\tau_{\mu}\circ\zeta_{v}
for
v\neq v_{1},v_{2}
is equivalent to the canonical neutralization, and such that
\tau_{\mu}\circ\zeta_{v_{1}}
is equivalent to
\xi_{\mu}
and
\tau_{\mu}\circ\zeta_{v_{2}}
is equivalent to
\xi_{-\mu}
(the definition of
\xi_{\mu}
appears after Theorem
2.2
). The homomorphism
\tau_{\mu}
is uniquely determined up to composition with an automorphism of
\mathscr{G}_{T}
that is equivalent to the identity automorphism at all places. The construction is functorial in
T
and
\mu
.

3.
The pseudomotivic Galois group

In this section, we introduce two gerbes: not only the truly important one, which we call the pseudomotivic Galois group, but also a subordinate one, which exists solely to be able to formulate the conjecture in the fifth section for the most general Shimura variety.

The pseudomotivic Galois group
\mathscr{P}
is defined as a direct limit. Let
L
be a finite Galois extension of
\mathbb{Q}
in the field
\overline{\mathbb{Q}}
of algebraic numbers in
\mathbb{C}
, and let
m
be a natural number. We first define a gerbe
\mathscr{P}(L,m)
, whose kernel is a torus
P(L,m)
, which we define by prescribing the Galois module of its characters. Let
q=p^{m}
, where
p
is the fixed rational prime.

Definition 3.1
.

The group
X(L,m)
of Weil numbers associated with
L
and
m
consists of those
\pi\in L
with the following properties:

(a)

There exists an integer
\nu_{1}=\nu_{1}(\pi)
such that for all Archimedean places
v
of
L
, we have

\Big|\prod_{\sigma\in{\rm Gal}(L_{v}/\mathbb{R})}\sigma\pi\Big|=q^{\nu_{1}}.

(b)

For each prime
v
of
L
above
p
, there exists an integer
\nu_{2}(v)=\nu_{2}(\pi,v)\in\mathbb{Z}
such that

|\pi|_{v}=\Big|\prod_{\sigma\in{\rm Gal}(L_{v}/\mathbb{Q}_{p})}\sigma\pi\Big|_{p}=q^{\nu_{2}(v)}.

(c)

At
†
†
margin:

141

all finite places outside
p
,
\pi
is a unit.

From Dirichlet’s Unit Theorem, it follows that
X(L,m)
is finitely generated. We divide out the finite group of the roots of unity contained in
X(L,m)
to obtain a torsion-free module
X^{*}(L,m)
. The corresponding torus is denoted by
P(L,m)
. The module
X^{*}(L,m)
is clearly a
{\rm Gal}(\overline{\mathbb{Q}}/\mathbb{Q})
-module. Consequently,
P(L,m)
is defined over
\mathbb{Q}
.

If necessary, to avoid misunderstandings, we denote by
\chi_{\pi}
the character of
P(L,m)
corresponding to the Weil number
\pi\in X(L,m)
. We fix an embedding
\overline{\mathbb{Q}}\to\overline{\mathbb{Q}}_{p}
once and for all, and consequently a prime place
v_{2}
of every finite-degree number field
L
contained in
\overline{\mathbb{Q}}
. Let
v_{1}
be the Archimedean place of
L
given by
L\hookrightarrow\overline{\mathbb{Q}}\hookrightarrow\mathbb{C}
. We define the cocharacters
\nu_{1},\nu_{2}
in
X_{*}(L,m)=X_{*}(P(L,m))
as follows:

(3.a)

\displaystyle\langle\nu_{1},\chi_{\pi}\rangle=\nu_{1}(\pi),

(3.b)

\displaystyle\langle\nu_{2},\chi_{\pi}\rangle=\nu_{2}(\pi,v_{2})

The relation (
2
) is obvious, while (
2
) follows from the product formula. It should be noted that this
\nu_{1}
is even invariant under the full group
{\rm Gal}(\overline{\mathbb{Q}}/\mathbb{Q})
.

The following functorialities are present:

(3.c) For
L\subset L^{\prime}
, we have
\phi^{*}=\phi^{*}_{L,L^{\prime}}:X^{*}(L,m)\to X^{*}(L^{\prime},m)
sending
\pi
to itself.

(3.d) For
m|m^{\prime}
, we have
\phi^{*}=\phi^{*}_{m,m^{\prime}}:X^{*}(L,m)\to X^{*}(L,m^{\prime})
sending
\pi
to
\pi^{m^{\prime}/m}
.

The contragredient maps are homomorphisms from the
\mathbb{Q}
-tori
P(L^{\prime},m)
and
P(L,m^{\prime})
into
P(L,m)
.

Lemma 3.2
.

(a)

We have

\phi_{m,m^{\prime}}(\nu_{i}^{\prime})=\nu_{i}.

(b)

We have

\phi_{L,L^{\prime}}(\nu_{i}^{\prime})=[L^{\prime}_{v_{i}^{\prime}}:L_{v_{i}}]\nu_{i},

where
v^{\prime}_{i}
are the places of
L^{\prime}
defined by
L^{\prime}\hookrightarrow\overline{\mathbb{Q}}\hookrightarrow\mathbb{C}
and
L^{\prime}\hookrightarrow\overline{\mathbb{Q}}\hookrightarrow\overline{\mathbb{Q}}_{p}
.

†
†
margin:

Proof of Lem.
3.2
.

If
m
is replaced by
m^{\prime}
, then
q
is replaced by
q^{m^{\prime}/m}
. From this, the first claim follows immediately. The second claim follows from the equation

\prod_{\sigma\in{\rm Gal}(L^{\prime}_{v_{i}^{\prime}}/\mathbb{Q}_{p})}\sigma\pi=\left(\prod_{\sigma\in{\rm Gal}(L_{v_{i}}/\mathbb{Q}_{p})}\sigma\pi\right)^{[L^{\prime}_{v_{i}^{\prime}}:L_{v_{i}}]},

which holds for
\pi\in L
.
†
†
margin:

QED Lem.
3.2
.

The
†
†
margin:

142

procedure from the previous section associates the gerbe
\mathscr{P}(L,m)
with the triple
P(L,m),\nu_{1},\nu_{2}
and the field
L
. From Lemma
3.2
(a), it follows that there is a canonical homomorphism

\phi=\phi_{m,m^{\prime}}:\mathscr{P}(L,m^{\prime})\to\mathscr{P}(L,m).

In the previous section, it was shown that the gerbe defined by
P(L,m),\nu_{1},\nu_{2}
, and
L
is isomorphic to the gerbe defined by
P(L,m),[L^{\prime}_{v_{1}^{\prime}}:L_{v_{1}}]\nu_{1},[L^{\prime}_{v_{2}^{\prime}}:L_{v_{2}}]\nu_{2}
, and
L^{\prime}
. Consequently, there is also a canonical homomorphism

\phi=\phi_{L,L^{\prime}}:\mathscr{P}(L^{\prime},m)\to\mathscr{P}(L,m).

As preparation for the next section, where we derive a canonical isomorphism between the pseudomotivic Galois group

\mathscr{P}=\varprojlim_{L,m}\mathscr{P}(L,m)

and the actual motivic Galois group based on the standard conjectures and the Tate conjecture, we need to demonstrate certain cohomological properties of the inverse system
P(L,m)
. Before doing so, we introduce a second, somewhat artificial gerb, which, for lack of a better name, we call the quasimotivic Galois group. It is defined using quasi-Weil numbers.

Definition 3.3
.

The group
Y(L,m)
of quasi-Weil numbers associated with
L
and
m
consists of those
\pi\in L
satisfying the following properties:

(a)

There exists an integer
\nu_{1}=\nu_{1}(\pi)
such that for all Archimedean places
v
of
L
,

\Big|\prod_{\sigma\in{\rm Gal}(L/\mathbb{Q})}\sigma\pi\Big|^{[L_{v}:\mathbb{R}]}=q^{\nu_{1}[L:\mathbb{Q}]}.

(b)

For each place
v
of
L
above
p
, there exists
\nu_{2}(v)=\nu_{2}(\pi,v)\in\mathbb{Z}
such that

|\pi|_{v}=\Big|\prod_{\sigma\in{\rm Gal}(L_{v}/\mathbb{Q}_{p})}\sigma\pi\Big|_{p}=q^{\nu_{2}(v)}.

(c)

At all finite prime places
v
outside
p
,
\pi
is a unit.

Just as
X(L,m)
, the groups
Y(L,m)
also form an inverse system. To obtain
Y^{*}(L,m)
, one divides
Y(L,m)
by the (typically infinite) group of all units, not just the roots of unity. This gives rise to an inverse system of gerbes
\mathscr{Q}(L,m)
, whose limit
\mathscr{Q}
we call the quasimotivic Galois group.

The group
P(L,m)
contains a sequence
\{\delta_{n}\}
, where
m|n
and
n
is sufficiently large, of distinguished rational points defined by the equations

\chi_{\pi}(\delta_{n})=\pi^{\frac{n}{m}},

where
\frac{n}{m}
must be divisible by the order of the torsion of
X(L,m)
. The rationality of
\delta_{n}
follows from the relation

\sigma(\chi_{\pi}(\delta_{n}))=(\sigma\pi)^{\frac{n}{m}}=\chi_{\sigma\pi}(\delta_{n}).

The equations
†
†
margin:

143

\phi_{m,m^{\prime}}(\delta_{n})=\delta_{n},\quad\phi_{L,L^{\prime}}(\delta_{n})=\delta_{n}

are easily verified. In
Q(L,m)
, the kernel of
\mathscr{Q}(L,m)
, there is no uniquely determined
\delta
. We can define several, and this lack of uniqueness will mostly be ignored. If
k
is sufficiently large, then there exists a commutative diagram of
{\rm Gal}(L/\mathbb{Q})
-modules:

\textstyle{Y(L,m)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{k\cdot Y^{*}(L,m)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\eta}
\textstyle{Y^{*}(L,m)}

We define
\delta_{n}
by the equations

\chi_{\pi}(\delta_{n})=\eta(\pi^{k})^{\frac{n}{mk}}.

Thus, for every
\pi
, the number
\pi^{-n}\chi_{\pi}(\delta_{n})
is a unit.

Let
T
be a torus defined over
\mathbb{Q}
, and let
\mu\in X_{*}(T)
. If
T
splits over
L
, and
m
is sufficiently large, we associate to the pair
(T,\mu)
a homomorphism

\psi_{\mu}:Q(L,m)\to T

defined over
\mathbb{Q}
.
1
1

1

This part of the article contains a well-known mistake. For the current definition of
Q(L,m)
, the map
\psi_{\mu}
cannot be constructed as claimed. The element
\pi_{\lambda}=\lambda(\gamma)
defined below does not satisfy Definition
3.3
(a) in general and hence is not an element of
Y(L,m)
. (If (
3.e
) holds, then
\pi_{\lambda}
is indeed an element of
X(L,m)\subset Y(L,m)
as claimed here, so we have
\psi_{\mu}:Q(L,m)\to T
factoring through
P(L,m)
.) For correction, one needs to appropriately modify the definition of
Q(L,m)
, and thereby modify the definition of the gerbe
\mathscr{Q}
. Such modifications have been proposed by Pfau
[
Pfa93
,
Pfa96
]
, and later by Reimann
[
Rei97
]
(see §B.2). (References are at the end of Translator’s Note.) The relationship between their versions is explained in Remark B2.13 of
loc. cit.
.
If

(3.e)

\displaystyle(\sigma-1)(\iota+1)\mu=(\iota+1)(\sigma-1)\mu=0,

then
\psi_{\mu}
can be factored through
P(L,m)
and can be regarded as a homomorphism from
P(L,m)
to
T
.
It should be noted that the embedding

X(L,m)\hookrightarrow Y(L,m)

defines a homomorphism

Q(L,m)\to P(L,m).

The homomorphism
\psi_{\mu}
is defined via a homomorphism of Galois modules
X^{*}(T)\to Y^{*}(L,m)
, specifically via
X^{*}(T)\to Y(L,m),\lambda\mapsto\pi_{\lambda}
, where it must hold that

\pi_{\sigma\lambda}=\sigma(\pi_{\lambda}).

If the equations (
3.e
) hold, then the following will also be true:

\sigma\big(\iota(\pi_{\lambda})\cdot\pi_{\lambda}\big)=\pi_{\sigma\iota\lambda+\sigma\lambda}=\pi_{(\iota+1)\sigma\lambda}=\pi_{(\iota+1)\lambda}=\iota(\pi_{\lambda})\cdot\pi_{\lambda},

and

\iota(\pi_{\lambda})\pi_{\lambda}=\pi_{\iota\lambda+\lambda}=\pi_{\iota^{\prime}\lambda+\lambda}=\iota^{\prime}(\pi_{\lambda})\pi_{\lambda},\quad\iota^{\prime}=\sigma\iota\sigma^{-1}.

Thus,
\iota(\pi_{\lambda})\pi_{\lambda}
is a rational number, and
\pi_{\lambda}\in X(L,m)
.

To

†
†
margin:

define
\lambda\mapsto\pi_{\lambda}

For the group

.

The homomorphism

must satisfy the conditions

2

According to the previous section,

Lemma 3.4
.

Let
K

Proof of Lem.

We

We now assume without loss of generality that
L

be its totally real subfield.

Lemma 3.5
.

H^{2}(\mathbb{Q},P(L,m))
satisfies the Hasse principle.

†

Proof of Lem.
3.5
.

Clearly, it suffices to show that the homomorphism

H^{1}\left({\rm Gal}(L^{\prime}/\mathbb{Q}),P(\mathbb A_{L^{\prime}})\right)\to H^{1}\left({\rm Gal}(L^{\prime}/\mathbb{Q}),X_{*}\otimes C_{L^{\prime}}\right)

is surjective when

and
L_{1}\subset L_{2}
. So, with Tate-Nakayama, we need to establish the surjectivity of

\bigoplus_{v\in\mathbb{Q}}H^{-1}\left({\rm Gal}(L_{v}^{\prime}/\mathbb{Q}_{v}),X_{*}\right)\to H^{-1}\left({\rm Gal}(L^{\prime}/\mathbb{Q}),X_{*}\right).

There is an exact sequence of Galois modules

1\to X_{*}^{\prime}\to X_{*}\to\mathbb{Z}\to 1,

where
\iota
acts as
-1
on
X_{*}^{\prime}
. An element from
H^{-1}({\rm Gal}(L^{\prime}/Q),X_{*})
is represented by
\lambda\in X^{*}
with

\sum_{\sigma\in{\rm Gal}(L^{\prime}/Q)}\sigma\lambda=0.

Consequently,
\lambda
lies in
X_{*}^{\prime}
, and

\sum_{\sigma\in{\rm Gal}(L^{\prime}_{v_{1}}/\mathbb{R})}\sigma\lambda=0,

so that
\lambda
represents an element from
H^{-1}({\rm Gal}(L^{\prime}_{v_{1}}/\mathbb{R}),X_{*})
. Thus, the lemma is proven.

There are still additional cohomological properties of the groups

. The Serre group

is a torus over
\mathbb{Q}
defined by its character module

:

X^{*}({}^{L}S)=\left\{\lambda=\sum_{\varrho:L\to\overline{\mathbb{Q}}}n_{\varrho}[\varrho]\mid n_{\varrho}+n_{\iota\varrho}\equiv k(\lambda),k(\lambda)\in\mathbb{Z}\right\}.

†
†
margin:

The group
{}^{L}S
is equipped with a distinguished cocharacter
\mu
:

\mu:\sum_{\varrho}n_{\varrho}[\varrho]\mapsto n_{1},

where

denotes the given embedding

. The cocharacter

defines
\psi_{\mu}:P(L,m)\to{}^{L}S
and

Lemma 3.6
.

For sufficiently large
m
, the homomorphism
\psi^{*}_{\mu}
is surjective, and thus

is injective.

To each
\varrho\in{\rm Gal}(L/\mathbb{Q})
, a prime

dividing

is assigned, with the condition:

|x|_{v_{\varrho}}=|\varrho^{-1}x|_{v_{2}}.

If
\sigma\in{\rm Gal}(L_{v_{2}}/\mathbb{Q}_{v_{2}})
, then
v_{\varrho\sigma}=v_{\varrho}
. The module

contains the distinguished elements
\nu_{\infty}=\nu_{1}
and
\nu_{\varrho}=-\varrho\nu_{2}
. The following relations hold:

\nu_{\varrho\sigma}=\nu_{\varrho},\quad\sigma\in{\rm Gal}(L_{v_{2}}/\mathbb{Q}_{v_{2}}),

and

\nu_{\varrho}+\nu_{\iota\varrho}=\nu_{\infty}k,\quad k=2[L_{v_{2}}:\mathbb{Q}_{p}][L:L_{0}]^{-1}.

The first relation is clear. The second follows from

q^{\nu_{\varrho}(\pi)+\nu_{\iota\varrho}(\pi)}=\Big|\prod_{\sigma\in{\rm Gal}(L_{v_{\varrho}}/\mathbb{Q}_{p})}\varrho^{-1}\sigma(\pi\iota(\pi))\Big|_{p}=|\pi\iota(\pi)|_{p}^{[L_{v_{2}}:\mathbb{Q}_{p}]}.

Lemma 3.7
.

(a)

p

Proof of Lem.

We use the Tate-Nakayama theory to prove that

for every

.

An element of

and the image

, where

in

The task is therefore to choose

such that

. This can be achieved by setting

, where

is a suitably chosen abelian extension of
\mathbb{Q}
.

The

Corollary 3.12
.

Let
L
be a CM-field. Then there exists a CM-extension

of

such that the transition homomorphism

H^{1}(\mathbb{Q},P_{L^{\prime}})\to H^{1}(\mathbb{Q},P_{L})

By Lemma

, there exists an embedding

\psi_{L}=\psi_{\mu}:\mathscr{P}_{L}\hookrightarrow\mathscr{G}_{{}^{L}S}.

From the definition of
\psi_{\mu}
and Lemma
3.7
, the commutativity of the following diagram (up to equivalence) follows:

(3.h)

\textstyle{\mathscr{P}_{L^{\prime}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\psi_{L^{\prime}}}
\textstyle{\mathscr{G}_{L^{\prime}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathscr{P}_{L}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}

\textstyle{\mathscr{G}_{L},}

where

and
\mathscr{G}_{L^{\prime}}=\mathscr{G}_{{}^{L^{\prime}}S}
. Furthermore, from the definitions in the previous section, the following hold:

(3.i)

\displaystyle\psi_{\mu}\circ\zeta_{v_{1}}\cong\xi_{\mu},\quad\psi_{\mu}\circ\zeta_{v_{2}}\cong\xi_{-\mu}.

(3.j) If
v
neither divides
\infty
nor

, then

is equivalent to the canonical neutralization of
\mathscr{G}_{L}
.

That the local homomorphisms are compatible with modifications of
L
, up to equivalence, follows from the results of the previous section.

Two local homomorphisms differ by an element in

H^{1}\left(\mathrm{Gal}(\overline{\mathbb{Q}}_{v}/\mathbb{Q}_{v}),{}^{L}S(\overline{\mathbb{Q}}_{v})\right).

The following lemma thus implies that, for a

of
\mathbb{Q}
, any two compatible families of local homomorphisms are automatically equivalent. In particular, the condition (
3
) as well as the second part of (

) are automatically satisfied.

Lemma 3.13
.

Let
v
be a non-archimedean place of
\mathbb{Q}
. For every CM-field
L
, there exists a CM-field
L^{\prime}
that contains
L
such that

H^{1}\left(\mathrm{Gal}(\overline{\mathbb{Q}}_{v}/\mathbb{Q}_{v}),{}^{L^{\prime}}S(\overline{\mathbb{Q}}_{v})\right)=0.

Proof.

We consider the exact sequence of algebraic tori over
\mathbb{Q}_{v}
([De2]):

1\to R_{L^{\prime}_{0}/\mathbb{Q}}\mathbb{G}_{m}\to\mathbb{G}_{m}\times R_{L^{\prime}/\mathbb{Q}}\mathbb{G}_{m}\to{}^{L^{\prime}}S\to 1.

†
†
margin:

150

The associated long exact sequence of cohomology gives:

0\to H^{1}(\mathbb{Q}_{v},{}^{L^{\prime}}S)\to\bigoplus_{v^{\prime}_{0}}\mathrm{Br}({L^{\prime}_{0}}_{v^{\prime}_{0}})\to\big(\bigoplus_{v^{\prime}}\mathrm{Br}(L^{\prime}_{v^{\prime}})\big)\oplus\mathrm{Br}(\mathbb{Q}_{v}).

The sums are taken over the places lying above
v
in the respective fields. The claim follows because we can find a Galois extension of CM type
L^{\prime}\supset L
such that all places in
L^{\prime}_{0}
above
v
split in
L^{\prime}
. Specifically, we choose a totally real Galois extension

of
L_{0}
that splits
L
at every place dividing

, and set

,
L^{\prime}=KL
. ∎

\displaystyle\phi_{\mu}(M_{L})=\psi_{\mu}(P_{L}).

We therefore identify

.

Assume that for every place

\textstyle{\mathscr{M}_{L^{\prime}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}

.

In the next section, we will need a statement that guarantees we can identify the gerbes
