margin:

QED Lem.
5.17
.

Lemma 5.18
.

\kappa(\gamma,\delta;\varepsilon^{\prime})=\kappa(\gamma,\delta;\varepsilon)
.

To make sense of this lemma, we must identify the groups in which the invariants lie. Let
\varepsilon^{\prime}=g^{-1}\varepsilon g
, with
g\in G_{\text{der}}(\overline{\mathbb{Q}})
. Then
u\mapsto u^{\prime}=g^{-1}ug
is an isomorphism from
G(\varepsilon)
to
G(\varepsilon^{\prime})
, which allows us to treat
G(\varepsilon^{\prime})
as an inner twist of
G(\varepsilon)
, since for every
\sigma\in\text{Gal}(\overline{\mathbb{Q}}/\mathbb{Q})
, the element
a_{\sigma}=g\sigma(g^{-1})
lies in
G(\varepsilon)
.
We can therefore identify
\widehat{G}(\varepsilon^{\prime})
with
\widehat{G}(\varepsilon)
as well as
Z(\varepsilon^{\prime})
with
Z(\varepsilon)
, and consequently
X^{\prime}/\prod_{v}X_{v}^{\prime}
with
X/\prod_{v}X_{v}
. We identify
G(\varepsilon^{\prime})
with
G(\varepsilon)
via the map
u\mapsto u^{\prime}
. This results in a new action of the Galois group on
G(\varepsilon)
, defined by the equations:

g^{-1}\sigma^{\prime}(u)g=\sigma(g^{-1}ug).

Thus we have
\sigma^{\prime}(u)=a_{\sigma}\sigma(u)a_{\sigma}^{-1}
.

To prove Lemma
5.18
, we require two additional lemmas, which we prove not only for
G(\varepsilon)
but also for any (reductive) group
G
. Let
\{a_{\sigma}\}
be a cocycle of
\text{Gal}(\overline{\mathbb{Q}}/\mathbb{Q})
with values in
G
, and let
G^{\prime}
be the corresponding inner twist. It is easy to verify that the map
\{v_{\sigma}\}\mapsto\{v^{\prime}_{\sigma}=v_{\sigma}a_{\sigma}^{-1}\}
defines a bijection
\xi:H^{1}(\mathbb{Q},G)\to H^{1}(\mathbb{Q},G^{\prime}),

as well as local bijections

\xi(v):H^{1}(\mathbb{Q}_{v},G)\to H^{1}(\mathbb{Q}_{v},G^{\prime}).

As above, we can identify
Z^{\prime}=Z(\widehat{G}^{\prime})
with
Z
, and consequently
\pi_{0}(Z^{\prime\Gamma(v)})^{D}
with
\pi_{0}(Z^{\Gamma(v)})^{D}
. The cocycle
\{a_{\sigma}\}
defines a class
\alpha\in H^{1}(\mathbb{Q},G)
with localizations
\alpha(v)
. Let
\delta(v)
be the element of
\pi_{0}(Z^{\Gamma(v)})^{D}
corresponding to
\alpha(v)
(see [K2], §6.4, for a finite place, and [K5], §1.2, for the general case), and
\eta(v)
the isomorphism arising from multiplication by
\delta(v)^{-1}
:

\pi_{0}(Z^{\Gamma(v)})^{D}\to\pi_{0}(Z^{\Gamma(v)})^{D}=\pi_{0}(Z^{\prime\Gamma(v)})^{D}.

Lemma 5.19
.

The diagram
†
†
margin:

187

\textstyle{H^{1}(\mathbb{Q}_{v},G)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\xi(v)}
\textstyle{H^{1}(\mathbb{Q}_{v},G^{\prime})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\pi_{0}(Z^{\Gamma(v)})^{D}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\eta(v)}
\textstyle{\pi_{0}(Z^{\prime\Gamma(v)})^{D}}

is commutative.

Proof of Lem.
5.19
.

We require a central extension

1\to Z\to H\to G\to 1,

with
H_{\text{der}}
simply connected, for which
H^{1}(\mathbb{Q}_{v},H)\to H^{1}(\mathbb{Q}_{v},G)
is surjective. For a non-archimedean place, such an extension is constructed in [K2], §6.4. For an archimedean place, any
z
-extension (see [K1], §1), for which
Z
is a torus induced from
\mathbb{C}
, has the desired property. It suffices to prove the lemma for
H
instead of
G
. Then, according to the definitions, we can replace
H
by
H_{\text{ab}}
, and for
H_{\text{ab}}
the claim is clear.

margin:

QED Lem.
5.19
.

The second lemma concerns an element
a\in G(\mathfrak{k})
such that for a sufficiently large
n
the product

a\sigma(a)\cdots\sigma^{n-1}(a)

is central in
G
, where
\sigma
is the Frobenius element. Then
a
defines a twist
G^{\prime}
of
G
, for which

G^{\prime}(\mathbb{Q}_{p})=\{g\in G(\mathfrak{k})\mid a\sigma(g)a^{-1}=g\}.

We have a bijection
\xi_{p}
from
B(G)
to
B(G^{\prime})
defined by

b\mapsto b^{\prime}=ba^{-1}.

We have

b^{\prime}\sigma^{\prime}(b^{\prime})\cdots\sigma^{\prime n-1}(b^{\prime})=b\sigma(b)\cdots\sigma^{n-1}(b)\sigma^{n-1}(a)^{-1}\cdots\sigma(a)^{-1}a^{-1}.

It then follows easily from [K4], Prop. 4.3.3, that
\xi_{p}
is also a bijection between
B(G)_{b}
and
B(G^{\prime})_{b}
. The element
\delta(p)
associated with
a
lies in
X^{*}(Z^{\Gamma(p)})
. By multiplying with
\delta(p)^{-1}
, we obtain an isomorphism

\eta(p):X^{*}(Z^{\Gamma(p)})\to X^{*}(Z^{\Gamma(p)})=X^{*}(Z^{\prime\Gamma(p)}).

Lemma 5.20
.

The diagram

\textstyle{B(G)_{b}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\xi(p)}
\textstyle{B(G^{\prime})_{b}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{X^{*}(Z^{\Gamma(p)})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\eta(p)}
\textstyle{X^{*}(Z^{\prime\Gamma(p)})}

is commutative.

Proof of Lem.
5.18
.

To prove Lemma
5.18
, we apply the two lemmas to

. According to Prop. 2.6 from [K5], the lemma would be proven if we could show that when we replace
\varepsilon
with
\varepsilon^{\prime}
,
\beta^{\prime}(v)
can be replaced by
\delta^{-1}(v)\beta^{\prime}(v)
.
Firstly, it should be noted that we had chosen
g\in G_{\text{der}}(\overline{\mathbb{Q}})
so that the cocycle
\{a_{\varrho}\}
lies in
G(\varepsilon)\cap G_{\text{der}}
, and secondly, it should be remembered that
Z(\varepsilon)/Z=Z(\widehat{H})
, where
H=G(\varepsilon)\cap G_{\text{der}}
.

When
v=l\neq p
, we have

\gamma=c\varepsilon c^{-1}=cg\varepsilon^{\prime}g^{-1}c^{-1}

and

g^{-1}c^{-1}\varrho(c)\varrho(g)=g^{-1}(b_{\varrho}a_{\varrho}^{-1})g.

Therefore, the class
\alpha(v)
of
\{b_{\varrho}\}
is replaced by
\xi(v)(\alpha(v))
. We can thus replace
\beta^{\prime}(v)
by
\delta^{-1}(v)\beta^{\prime}(v)
.

At the place
p
, we have

\text{Nm}_{L_{n}/\mathbb{Q}_{p}}(\delta)=cg\varepsilon^{\prime}g^{-1}c^{-1}.

Unfortunately,
g
may not lie in
G_{\text{der}}(\mathbb{Q}_{p}^{\text{un}})
. However, it can be written as a product
g=kg_{1}
with
g_{1}\in G_{\text{der}}(\mathbb{Q}_{p}^{\text{un}})
and
k\in G_{\text{der}}(\overline{\mathbb{Q}}_{p})\cap G(\varepsilon)
.

Thus we have

g\varrho(g)^{-1}=kg_{1}\varrho(g_{1})^{-1}\varrho(k)^{-1},

so
g
and
g_{1}
give the same class in
H^{1}(\mathbb{Q}_{p},G(\varepsilon)\cap G_{\text{der}})
. The identification of
\widehat{G}(\varepsilon^{\prime})
and
\widehat{G}(\varepsilon)
defined by
g
is also the one defined by
g_{1}
. Therefore, we can replace
g
with
g_{1}
if we want to show that
\beta^{\prime}(v)
can be replaced by
\delta^{-1}(v)\beta^{\prime}(v)
.

We have

\text{Nm}_{L_{n}/\mathbb{Q}_{p}}(\delta)=cg_{1}\varepsilon^{\prime}g_{1}^{-1}c^{-1}.

Thus, we replace
b
with

b^{\prime}=g_{1}^{-1}\left(b{\sigma}(g_{1})g_{1}^{-1}\right)g_{1},

so that the desired replacement follows from Lemma
5.19
and the compatibility ([K4]) of the two bijections
B(G)_{b}\to X^{*}(Z^{\Gamma(p)})
and
H^{1}(\mathbb{Q}_{p},G)\to\pi_{0}(Z^{\Gamma(p)})^{D}
.

For
†
†
margin:

189

the infinite case, the claim that

is local and transitive. It holds when

and
\varepsilon^{\prime}
are conjugated within

. Hence, we can assume that there is a Cartan subgroup

of

.

QED Lem.
5.18
.

We now return to our presumed description of points on a reduced Shimura variety. The conditions of Theorem
5.3
are assumed to hold. Let
m
be a given natural number. We consider pairs
(\phi,\varepsilon)
with the following properties:

(i)

\phi:\mathscr{Q}\to\mathscr{G}_{G}
is admissible.

(ii)

\varepsilon\in I_{\phi}
.

(iii)

There exist
\gamma=(\gamma(l))\in G(\mathbb A_{f}^{p})

, and

in the

-orbit of the point

Such pairs are called

. It is not required that

lies in

. If

, where

. Therefore, the conjugacy class of

is uniquely determined by the pair

. Since

is a twisted form of a subgroup of

\varepsilon
is stably conjugate to an element of
G
. In particular, it is stably conjugate to each
\gamma(l)
. It also follows from Lemma
5.23
below that

is stably conjugate to an element

\gamma(l)
. It follows easily from the definition of

and Greenberg’s Lemma [G] that

are conjugate almost everywhere.

To define

with

and introduced

, where

in order to obtain its action on

and also for the condition

on

.
According to Lemma 1.4.9 from [K3], condition (iii) implies the existence of

and

such that

and
\text{Nm}_{L_{n}/\mathbb{Q}_{p}}\delta=c\varepsilon^{\prime}c^{-1}=cu\varepsilon u^{-1}c^{-1}=cug\varepsilon_{0}g^{-1}u^{-1}c^{-1},
if
\varepsilon=g\varepsilon_{0}g^{-1}.

Thus, the triple
\gamma,\delta,\varepsilon_{0}
satisfies conditions (ii) and (iii) (where
\varepsilon
is replaced by
\varepsilon_{0}
), which were used to define the

-invariant. That condition (i) is also satisfied follows immediately from (

follows from (

and

define the same class in

. The condition

is also satisfied, as follows from Theorem

. We can therefore introduce the invariant
\kappa(\gamma,\delta;\varepsilon_{0})
. We immediately emphasize that the
b
that appears in the definition of the invariants is the same as the one that appears in
F=b\times\sigma
.

Theorem
5.21
below describes those
\varepsilon_{0}
for which there exists an admissible pair
(\phi,\varepsilon)
with
\varepsilon
stably conjugate to
\varepsilon_{0}
. Two admissible pairs

and

are called

such that

\phi^{\prime}=\mathrm{ad}\,g\circ\phi,\varepsilon^{\prime}=\mathrm{ad}\,g(\varepsilon).

Theorem

counts the classes of

with

We now come to the necessity of condition

can only commute with
\Phi^{tm}
if it also commutes with
\sigma^{tn}
and
p^{-t\nu_{2}}
.

From the equation
\varepsilon^{\prime}x=\Phi^{m}x
, it generally follows that

(\varepsilon^{\prime})^{t}x=\Phi^{tm}x,\quad t\in\mathbb{Z}.

Let
t
be large enough such that
\sigma^{t}(x)=x
. Then
k^{\prime}=p^{t^{\prime}\nu_{2}}\cdot(\varepsilon^{\prime})^{t}
has a fixed point, and its eigenvalues all have absolute value 1.

The decomposition

(\varepsilon^{\prime})^{t}=p^{-t^{\prime}\nu_{2}}k^{\prime}

is a kind of polar decomposition of
(\varepsilon^{\prime})^{t}
and is uniquely determined. From the uniqueness, it follows that
p^{-t^{\prime}\nu_{2}}
lies in the center of
G(\varepsilon)
. Since
\mathscr{J}
is the centralizer of
p^{-t^{\prime}\nu_{2}}
and
p^{-t^{\prime}\nu_{2}}
obviously generates a one-dimensional splitting torus, it follows that
M
is contained in
\mathscr{J}
.

From
†
†
margin:

192

[K3], §3, it follows easily that

\lambda_{M}(\varepsilon)=\frac{1}{t}\lambda_{M}(\varepsilon^{t})=\frac{1}{t}\lambda_{M}(p^{-t^{\prime}\nu_{2}})=-\frac{t^{\prime}}{t}\nu_{2},

and the restriction of
-\frac{t^{\prime}}{t}\nu_{2}
to
Z(\widehat{M})\subset\widehat{T}
is equal to
\text{Nm}_{L_{n}/\mathbb{Q}_{p}}\mu_{1}^{\prime}
. Although
\mu^{\prime}
itself may not be defined over
L_{n}
, by the definition of
E
and Lemma 1.1.3 from [K3], there exists a
\mu:\mathbb G_{m}\to M
conjugate to
\mu^{\prime}
and defined over
L_{n}
. It is clear that
\mu_{1}=\mu_{1}^{\prime}
.

We now assume that
\varepsilon_{0}
satisfies the two conditions of Theorem
5.21
and prove the existence of an admissible pair
(\phi,\varepsilon)
with
\varepsilon
stably conjugate to
\varepsilon_{0}
. We can find a Cartan subgroup
T
of
G(\varepsilon_{0})
defined over
\mathbb{Q}
and a cocharacter
\mu
of
T
such that
T
is elliptic at infinity and at
p
, and such that
\mu
, though not necessarily the homomorphism from
\mathbb G_{m}
to
M
required by assumption (b), is at least conjugate to it in
M
. It might be the case that
\psi_{T,\mu}
is not admissible. In that case, we use the proof of Lemma
5.12
to find a
u\in G(\overline{\mathbb{Q}})
such that
T^{\prime}=uTu^{-1}
and
t\mapsto t^{\prime}=utu^{-1}
are both defined over
\mathbb{Q}
, while
\mu^{\prime}=\mathrm{ad}\,u\circ\mu=\mu_{h},h\in X_{\infty}
. If necessary, we replace
\varepsilon_{0}
by
\varepsilon^{\prime}_{0}=\mathrm{ad}\,u(\varepsilon_{0})
and
T,\mu
by
T^{\prime},\mu^{\prime}
, so that
\psi_{T,\mu}
becomes admissible.
Then
X^{p}
contains a point
y
from
\prod_{l\neq p}T(\overline{\mathbb{Q}}_{l})
, which necessarily commutes with
\varepsilon_{0}
, and so
\varepsilon_{0}y=y\varepsilon_{0}
. Consequently, the pair
(\phi,\varepsilon)=(\psi_{T,\mu},\varepsilon_{0})
satisfies the conditions (i), (ii), and the part of (iii) about the existence of the point
y
.

To verify the second part of (iii), we can assume that
\xi^{\prime}_{p}
, which defines
X_{p}
and
\Phi
, is equal to
\mathrm{ad}\,u\circ\xi_{p}=\xi_{-\mu^{\prime}}
, where
u\in M(\overline{\mathbb{Q}}_{p})
, and where
\xi_{-\mu^{\prime}}
corresponds to an elliptic Cartan subgroup
T^{\prime}
of
M
split over
\mathfrak{k}
and a cocharacter
\mu^{\prime}
conjugate to
\mu
. Let
\varepsilon^{\prime}=\mathrm{ad}\,u(\varepsilon)
. Then

\Phi^{m}=p^{\nu}\times\sigma^{n}

with

\nu=\sum_{j=0}^{n-1}\sigma^{j}(\mu^{\prime}).

By assumption
*(\varepsilon)
, we have

\lambda_{M}(\varepsilon^{\prime})=\lambda_{M}(\varepsilon)=\lambda_{M}(p^{\nu}).

Since the cocharacter of
M_{\mathrm{ab}}
defined by
\mu^{\prime}
is defined over
\mathbb{Q}_{p}
, we conclude that the image of
(\varepsilon^{\prime})^{-1}p^{\nu}
lies in the maximal compact subgroup of
M_{\mathrm{ab}}(\mathfrak{k})
. Therefore, there exists
c\in T^{\prime}(\mathfrak{k})
such that
c^{-1}(\varepsilon^{\prime})^{-1}\Phi^{m}c\in M_{\mathrm{sc}}(\mathfrak{k})\times\sigma^{n}
.

To prove the existence of
x
, we must (see [K3], Lemma 1.4.9) show the existence of a
u\in G(\mathfrak{k})
such that
u^{-1}(\varepsilon^{\prime})^{-1}\Phi^{m}u=\sigma^{n}
. Instead, we seek something stronger: a
d\in M_{\mathrm{sc}}(\mathfrak{k})
such that
d^{-1}c^{-1}(\varepsilon^{\prime})^{-1}\Phi^{m}cd=\sigma^{n}
.

It
†
†
margin:

193

is sufficient to show that
c^{-1}\varepsilon^{\prime-1}\Phi^{m}c\sigma^{-n}\in M_{\mathrm{sc}}(\mathfrak{k})
is basic, since Prop. 5.4 of [K4] states that
B(M_{\mathrm{sc}})_{b}
is trivial over any finite extension of
\mathbb{Q}_{p}
, in particular over
L_{n}
. According to condition (4.3.3) of [K4],
c^{-1}\varepsilon^{\prime-1}\Phi^{m}c\sigma^{-n}
is basic if for sufficiently large
t
,

(5.q)

\displaystyle c^{-1}(\varepsilon^{\prime-1}\Phi^{m})^{t}c=e\sigma^{tn}e^{-1}.

For a large
r
, we have
\Phi^{mr}=p^{\nu^{\prime}}\times\sigma^{rn}
with

\nu^{\prime}=\sum_{j=0}^{rn-1}\sigma^{j}(\mu),

and
\varepsilon^{\prime}
commutes with
p^{\nu^{\prime}}
and
\sigma^{rn}
. Furthermore,
\nu^{\prime}
is central, and
(\varepsilon^{\prime})^{r}
has a polar decomposition
(\varepsilon^{\prime})^{r}=p^{\nu^{\prime}}k
.
Therefore,

(\varepsilon^{\prime-1}\Phi^{m})^{sr}=k^{s}\times\sigma^{srn},

and

c^{-1}(\varepsilon^{\prime-1}\Phi^{m})^{sr}c=c^{-1}k^{-s}\sigma^{srn}(c)\times\sigma^{srn}=(c^{-1}k^{-s}c)(c^{-1}\sigma^{srn}(c))\times\sigma^{srn}.

For large
s
and
r
,
k^{-s}
and
c^{-1}\sigma^{srn}(c)
are inside an arbitrarily given neighborhood of
1
. Thus, the existence of a solution to (
5.q
) is proven and thus Theorem
5.21
.

QED Thm.

.

In Theorem

below, an invariant

appears, namely the order of the kernel of

If

Lemma 5.24
.

Let

be a reductive group over
\mathbb{Q}
without an

-factor,

and

H^{1}(\mathbb{Q},G^{\prime})\to H^{1}(\mathbb{Q},A)\times\prod_{v}H^{1}(\mathbb{Q}_{v},G^{\prime})

are equal.

.

As in the proof of Lemma 4.3.2 from [K2], we can replace

have already noted that the conjugacy class of

The
†

margin:

195

Thus, the claim is also proved at

, and the equation

We fix an admissible and well-positioned pair

Lemma 5.26
.

(\phi,\varepsilon_{0})

.

\alpha(v)

The proof is deferred. From this, it follows that

Note.

\mathscr{D}^{n}_{\mathfrak{k}}
. Therefore, the homomorphisms
\mathscr{D}^{mn}\to\mathscr{D}^{n}
(resp.
\mathscr{D}^{mn}_{\mathfrak{k}}\to\mathscr{D}^{n}_{\mathfrak{k}}
), which map
x\mapsto x^{m}
for
x\in(\mathbb{Q}^{\mathrm{un}}_{p})^{\times}
(resp.
\mathfrak{k}^{\times}
), and
d_{\sigma}\mapsto d_{\sigma}
, form a compatible family. In
D^{n}_{\mathfrak{k}}
, each element
xd_{\sigma}
with
x\in\mathfrak{k}^{\times},|x|=1
is conjugate to
d_{\sigma}
.

Any homomorphism
\xi_{p}:\mathscr{D}\to\mathscr{G}_{G}
is equivalent, by Steinberg’s theorem (see [Se], p. 3), to a homomorphism
\xi^{\prime}_{p}
that can be induced from

\xi^{\prime}_{p}:\mathscr{D}_{n}\to G(\mathbb{Q}^{\mathrm{un}}_{p})\rtimes\text{Gal}(\mathbb{Q}^{\mathrm{un}}_{p}/\mathbb{Q}_{p}).

Let us assume that
\xi_{p}
itself can be defined in this way.

The element
\xi(w)
that appears in the definition of
X_{p}
is nothing other than
\xi_{p}(d_{\sigma})
. In general,
\xi_{p}(d_{\sigma})=b\times\sigma
, and
b
defines a class in
B(G)
, which depends only on the class of
\xi_{p}
. If we choose
n
sufficiently large, the
n\nu
appearing in [K4], equation (4.3.3), is the restriction of
\zeta^{-1}_{p}
on
(\mathbb{Q}^{\mathrm{un}}_{p})^{\times}\subset\mathscr{D}^{n}
, which is a homomorphism of algebraic groups. We obtain in this way a map from
C(G)
, the set of classes in
\text{Hom}(\mathscr{D},\mathscr{G}_{G})
, to
B(G)
. Let
C(G)_{b}
be the preimage of
B(G)_{b}
. We do not know whether
C(G)\to B(G)
is bijective. However, it follows easily from [K4] that
C(G)_{b}\to B(G)_{b}
is bijective.

For tori, one has the functor
11
11

11

Here the two appearances of “functor” should be understood as “natural transformation”.

C(T)\to B(T)
, as well as the functor
\mu\mapsto\xi_{-\mu}
from
X_{*}(T)
to
C(T)
. Since
\zeta_{-\mu}(d_{\sigma})=p^{\mu}\times\sigma
, the composition maps
1\in X_{*}(\mathbb G_{m})
to
b=p
. It therefore follows from Lemma 2.2 of [K4] that the composition is an isomorphism. Consequently,
C(T)\to B(T)
is surjective. It is injective because the number of classes in
C(T)
(resp.
B(T)
) for a given
\nu
is equal to
|H^{1}(\mathbb{Q}_{p},T)|
. For a general group, injectivity is also proved in this way. Surjectivity follows from [K4], Prop. 5.3.

6.
The conjecture as a consequence of the Standard Conjectures in some cases

†
†
margin:

198

In this section, we want to show that, for certain Shimura varieties, the conjecture in §5 follows from the identification of the pseudo-motivic Galois group and the motivic Galois group, which was carried out in §4 under the assumption of the standard conjectures, the Tate conjecture, and the Hodge conjecture. In doing so, we closely follow Zink’s work [Z2], so the reader is expected to have at least a superficial familiarity with this work.
We begin with the definition of the relevant Shimura varieties and recall some group-theoretic facts, for whose proofs we refer to [De3] and [Z2].

Let
D
be a simple, finite-dimensional
\mathbb{Q}
-algebra of degree
d^{2}
over its center
L
, equipped with a positive involution
x\mapsto x^{*}
. Let
L_{0}
be the field of invariants of the involution in
L
. Let
(V,\psi)
be a symplectic
D
-module, i.e., a
D
-module equipped with a non-degenerate alternating
\mathbb{Q}
-bilinear form such that

\psi(xu,v)=\psi(u,x^{*}v),\quad x\in D,\quad u,v\in V.

Let
\dim_{L}V=d\cdot m
and
[L_{0}:\mathbb{Q}]=n
. We assume that
(D,*)
is of one of the following two types:

(A) The involution is of the second kind, i.e., it induces a nontrivial automorphism of
L
. In this case,
L_{0}
is a totally real number field.

(C) The involution is of the first kind, i.e., it induces the trivial automorphism on
L
. If
K\supset L
is a splitting field of
D
, then we find an isomorphism
D\otimes_{L}K\cong M_{d}(K)
and a
K
-linear extension of the involution. In matrix notation, we assume
x^{*}=c\,^{t}xc^{-1}
with
{}^{t}c=c
. In this case,
L
is a totally real number field.

Let
G
be the group of symplectic
L_{0}
-similarities, regarded as an algebraic group over
\mathbb{Q}
:

G=\{g\in{\rm GL}_{D}(V)\mid\psi(gu,gv)=\psi(c(g)u,v),\quad c(g)\in L_{0}^{\times}\}.

This is a connected reductive group over
\mathbb{Q}
, whose derived group is simply connected ([De3], 5.8). (In case (A),
G_{\mathrm{der}}
is a product of special unitary groups, and in case (C), a product of symplectic groups). The groups
G
and
G_{\text{der}}
satisfy the Hasse principle ([De3], 5.11). Furthermore, there exists a homomorphism

h:\mathbb S\to G_{\mathbb{R}},

which, up to conjugation, is characterized by the fact that the Hodge structure induced on
V
is of type
(+1,0)+(0,+1)
, and that
\psi(u,h(i)v)
is symmetric and positive definite ([Z2], 3.1). The pair
(G,h)
defines a Shimura variety, where it should be noted that the weight homomorphism
\mu+\bar{\mu}:\mathbb{G}_{m}\to G

is defined over
\mathbb{Q}
and that
X_{*}(G_{\text{ab}})
satisfies the Serre condition (
3.e
). The
D
-module
V
is uniquely determined up to isomorphism by

t(x)=\text{Tr}(x\mid V_{h}^{1,0}),\quad x\in D,

(see [De3], 5.10). Let
†
†
margin:

199

E=E(G,h)=\mathbb{Q}(t(x)\mid x\in D).

This is the Shimura field. (In case (C),
E=\mathbb{Q}
.) Let
K\subset G(\mathbb A_{f})
be an open compact subgroup. The Shimura variety
\text{Sh}_{K}(G,h)
has a canonical model over
E
and is a
coarse
moduli scheme for the moduli problem on
(\text{Sch}/E)
, which associates to an
E
-scheme
S
the following data:

(6.a)

(i)

An abelian
D
-scheme up to isogeny
(A,\iota:D\to\mathrm{End}\,A)
such that

\mathrm{Tr}(x\mid\mathrm{Lie}^{*}A)=t(x),\quad x\in D.

Here,
\mathrm{Lie}^{*}A
denotes the cotangent space of
A
.

(ii)

An
L_{0}
-homogeneous polarization
\Lambda
, which induces the involution
*
on
D
.

(iii)

A class of
D
-module isomorphisms of sheaves for the étale topology

\bar{\eta}:H^{1}(A,\mathbb A_{f})V\otimes\mathbb A_{f}\mod K,

which preserves the bilinear forms on both sides up to a constant in
L_{0}\otimes\mathbb A_{f}
.

Here,
H^{1}(A,\mathbb A_{f})=\prod H^{1}(A,\mathbb{Q}_{l})
denotes the
l
-adic cohomology with coefficients in
\mathbb A_{f}
.

We now fix our prime number
p
. Let
O_{D}
be an order in

and
V_{\mathbb{Z}}
an

-invariant lattice in

, such that

(6.1)

p
is unramified in

is a product of matrix algebras, and
O_{D}\otimes\mathbb{Z}_{p}
is a maximal order,

c)

\psi:V_{\mathbb{Z}_{p}}\times V_{\mathbb{Z}_{p}}\to\mathbb{Z}_{p}
is a perfect pairing.

Then we have
*(O_{D}\otimes\mathbb{Z}_{p})=O_{D}\otimes\mathbb{Z}_{p}
. Let

K_{p}=G(\mathbb{Q}_{p})\cap\text{End}_{O_{D}}V_{\mathbb{Z}_{p}}.

Then
G
is quasi-split over
\mathbb{Q}_{p}
and split over an unramified extension, and

is
the group of integer points, i.e.([T], 3.8) a hyperspecial subgroup. Let

To define a model of

over

We refer to [Z2], 3.5, for a proof of the fact that the moduli problems (

Theorem 6.2
.

The moduli problem (
6
) admits a coarse moduli scheme over
\mathrm{Spec}\,(\mathcal{O}_{\mathfrak{p}})
. If
K^{p}
is sufficiently small, then the moduli scheme is smooth, and if
L_{0}=\mathbb{Q}
, it is also a fine moduli scheme.

We outline a proof, which follows the proof of the corresponding Theorem 1.7 from [Z2]. The
O_{D}
-lattice
\eta^{-1}(V_{\mathbb{Z}}\otimes\widehat{\mathbb{Z}}^{p})\subset H^{1}(A,\mathbb A_{f}^{p})
is independent of the choice of

and defines an abelian

in the isogeny class of

, equipped with an

\bar{\eta}:H^{1}(B,\widehat{\mathbb{Z}}^{p})\cong V_{\mathbb{Z}}\otimes\widehat{\mathbb{Z}}^{p}\mod K^{p}.

This shows that the moduli problem (
6
) is equivalent to the following moduli problem.

(6.c)

(i)

An abelian
O_{D}
-scheme
B
, such that

\mathrm{Tr}(x\mid\mathrm{Lie}^{*}B)=t(x),\quad x\in O_{D}.

(ii)

As in (
6
), (ii).

(iii)

A class of
O_{D}
-module isomorphisms

\bar{\eta}:H^{1}(B,\widehat{\mathbb{Z}}^{p})V_{\mathbb{Z}}\otimes\widehat{\mathbb{Z}}^{p}\mod K^{p},

preserving the bilinear forms on both sides up to a constant in
L_{0}\otimes\mathbb A_{f}^{p}
.

By using the methods of Mumford [Mu2] (or those of Artin [A], if one is satisfied with the construction of an algebraic space), we obtain the existence of a fine moduli scheme if in (ii) we require the presence of an effective involution-preserving polarization and if
K^{p}
is taken to be sufficiently small.
If
(B,\Lambda,\bar{\eta})
is a
T
-valued point of the moduli problem (
6
) and
\lambda\in\Lambda
is prime to

and
\eta\in\bar{\eta}
, then, if

denotes the associated Riemann form,

\psi\big(\eta(x),\eta(y)\big)=E^{\lambda}(tx,y),\quad x,y\in H^{1}(B,\widehat{\mathbb{Z}}^{p})

for some

.
The residue class
\tau(B,c,\lambda,\bar{\eta})
of
t
in the
\text{Gal}(\overline{\mathbb{Q}}_{p}/E_{\mathfrak{p}})
-module

is independent of the choice of
\eta\in\bar{\eta}
and can be regarded as a section of this sheaf over
T
.
Two polarizations
\lambda
and
\lambda^{\prime}
differ by a totally positive element of
L_{0}
that is a unit at
p
. Thus, we obtain a section
†
†
margin:

201

of
L_{0}^{+}\backslash L_{0}^{\times}(\mathbb A_{f})/c(K)\,(+1)

Let
\tau_{1},\dots,\tau_{r}
be sections of

over a finite unramified extension

of

(6.d)

(i)

As in (

(ii)

An involution-preserving polarization

.

(iii)

.

If we choose the

such that they are representatives of

is an effective polarization whose degree is determined by

. Consequently, according to Mumford, the moduli problem (

is representable by a quasi-projective

. The group
L_{0}^{+}\cap c(K)
acts on

(viewed as an

-scheme) by changing the polarization. It is easy to verify that this action factors over the finite quotient

, and that the quotient is a coarse moduli scheme for the modular problem (
6

\mathrm{Spec}\,\mathcal{O}_{\mathfrak{p}}

, the action of

is free. Let

\alpha:(B,\iota,\lambda,\bar{\eta})(B,\iota,\zeta\lambda,\bar{\eta})

be an isomorphism. We must show that
\zeta\in c(L^{\times}\cap K)
. Let
K^{\prime}=K^{\prime p}\cdot K_{p}
be a sufficiently small open compact subgroup. We consider only

, and denote the equivalence class of

\bar{\eta}^{\prime}

Let

. Then,

, and

coincides with

\zeta_{1}\in K
, implying

To convince ourselves of the smoothness of

Its

It must be shown that this filtration can be lifted to a totally isotropic filtration of

In case (C),
L_{0}=L

Then the

The case (A) is handled analogously (cf. [Z2], 3.4).

With this, our sketch of the proof of Theorem

Let

In terms of the moduli problem (

is one that respects the actions of

Theorem 6.3
.

The set of isogeny classes of points of
\Sh(\bar{\kappa})
is in a one-to-one correspondence with the equivalence classes of admissible homomorphisms
\phi:\mathscr{P}\to\mathscr{G}_{G}
.

We assume, however, that
\mathscr{P}\cong\mathscr{M}
in
\mathscr{G}_{S}
. According to the definition of
\mathscr{M}
, the homogeneous motive
M^{1}(A)
of degree 1 associated with an abelian variety corresponds to a homomorphism
\phi:\mathscr{M}\to\mathscr{G}_{V^{\prime}}
. Since

\text{Hom}(M^{1}(A),M^{1}(A^{\prime}))\cong\text{Hom}{Q}(A,A^{\prime}),

A
is defined as an abelian D-variety by an embedding
\iota^{\prime}:D\to\text{End}\,V^{\prime}
that is compatible with
\phi
. Similarly, the dual variety
A^{*}
is defined by the contragredient homomorphism
\phi^{*}
on
V^{\prime*}
. For
T
the Tate motive, we have
V^{\prime*}=\text{Hom}(V^{\prime},T)
. A polarization corresponds to a homomorphism
\psi:V^{\prime}\to V^{\prime*}
that is compatible with
\phi
and
\phi^{*}
, i.e., a bilinear form
\psi^{\prime}
on
V^{\prime}
for which the equation

\psi^{\prime}(\phi(g)u,\phi(g)v)=\tau(g)\cdot\psi^{\prime}(u,v)

holds, where
\tau
denotes the action corresponding to
T
on
\overline{\mathbb{Q}}
. The isomorphism class of
(A,\iota,\lambda)
is clearly determined by
(V^{\prime},\iota^{\prime},\psi^{\prime})
. Due to the existence of a normal form, we can assume that
(V^{\prime},\iota^{\prime},\psi^{\prime})=(V,\iota,\psi)
. Then, the image of
\mathscr{M}
lies in
\mathscr{G}_{G_{0}}\subset\mathscr{G}_{V}
. Two homomorphisms
\phi_{1},\phi_{2}:\mathscr{M}\to\mathscr{G}_{G_{0}}
are equivalent under
G(\overline{\mathbb{Q}})
if and only if
(A_{1},\iota_{1},\lambda_{1})\cong(A_{2},\iota_{2},c\lambda_{2}),c\in L_{0}^{\times}
. We have
G^{0}_{\text{ab}}=\mathbb{Q}^{\times}\subset G_{\text{ab}}=L_{0}^{\times}
. Since the cocharacter
\mu_{\text{ab}}
factors through
G^{0}_{\text{ab}}
, every admissible
\phi:\mathscr{Q}\to\mathscr{G}_{G}
factors through
\mathscr{G}_{G_{0}}
. Since
X_{*}(G_{\text{ab}})
satisfies the Serre condition (
3.e
),
\phi
also factors through
\mathscr{P}=\mathscr{M}
.

To complete the proof, it suffices to show that if
(A,\iota,\Lambda)
arises from
(A,\iota,\Lambda,\bar{\eta})
, then the corresponding
\phi
is admissible, and vice versa. This is not difficult. However, we prefer a different approach that directly associates an admissible
\phi
to each
(A,\iota,\Lambda,\bar{\eta})
and vice versa, without assuming any unproven conjectures, though we do not at present prove the bijectivity of this correspondence. Furthermore, it is not straightforward to apply this if
G
is not quasi-split over
\mathbb{Q}_{p}
(see the second example in §7).

Since
†
†
margin:

204

A
is already defined over a finite field, it is an abelian variety of CM type, and we can consider the point
(A,\iota,\Lambda,\bar{\eta})
as a special point
(A,\iota,\Lambda,\bar{\eta},R,\vartheta)
. According to Zink’s lifting theorem [Z2], §4.4, there exists a mixed characteristic discrete valuation ring
\mathcal{O}
with residue field
\bar{\kappa}
, and a special point
(\widetilde{A},\widetilde{\iota},\widetilde{\Lambda},\widetilde{\bar{\eta}},R,\widetilde{\vartheta})
, whose reduction is isogenous to the original point. Let
(T,\mu)
be the corresponding torus with cocharacter in
G
. The homomorphism
\psi_{T,\mu}:\mathscr{P}\to\mathscr{G}_{S}\to\mathscr{G}_{T}\subset\mathscr{G}_{G}
is admissible. Since
\phi\cong\psi_{T,\mu}
, it follows that
\phi
is admissible. Conversely, if
\phi
is admissible, it is equivalent to
\psi_{T,\mu}
. It defines
\psi_{T,\mu}
and
(\widetilde{A},\widetilde{\iota},\widetilde{\Lambda},\widetilde{\bar{\eta}},R,\widetilde{\vartheta})
, which by reduction yields
(A,\iota,\Lambda,\bar{\eta})
.

In the following we fix an isogeny class
\mathscr{J}=(A,\iota,\Lambda){Q}
and the corresponding admissible homomorphism
\phi
up to equivalence. We denote by
\mathscr{J}_{K}
the set of points of
\Sh_{K}(\mathbb{F})
in
\mathscr{J}
. Let
r=[E_{\mathfrak{p}}:\mathbb{Q}_{p}]
, and
q=p^{r}
be the number of elements in
\kappa
. If
(A,\iota,\Lambda,\bar{\eta})
is a point of
\Sh_{K}(\mathbb{F})
, then the inverse image
(A^{(q)},\iota^{(q)},\Lambda^{(q)},\bar{\eta}^{(q)})
under the
r
-th power of the Frobenius is again a point of
\Sh_{K}(\mathbb{F})
. The existence of the relative Frobenius

\Fr^{r}:(A,\iota,\Lambda,\bar{\eta})\to(A^{(q)},\iota^{(q)},\Lambda^{(q)},\bar{\eta}^{(q)})

shows that we obtain an action of
\text{Gal}(\mathbb{F}/\kappa)
on
\mathscr{J}_{K}
, which is also obviously compatible with the change of
K^{p}
.

Theorem 6.4
.

There are bijections

\mathscr{J}_{K}\cong X_{\phi}(K),

such that the action of
\Phi
on the right hand set (see (
5
)) corresponds to the action of the Frobenius in
\text{Gal}(\mathbb{F}/\kappa)
on the left hand set, and they are compatible with the change of
K^{p}
.

In the following we fix a point
(A_{0},\iota_{0},\Lambda_{0},\bar{\eta}_{0})
in
\mathscr{J}_{K}
, which is the reduction modulo
p
of a special point
(\widetilde{A}_{0},\widetilde{\iota}_{0},\widetilde{\Lambda}_{0},\widetilde{\bar{\eta}}_{0})
and defines, as in the proof of
6.2
, the torus
(T,\mu)
.

An isogeny decomposes into a chain of a prime-to-
p
isogeny and a
p
-power isogeny. If an element
\eta_{0}\in\bar{\eta}_{0}
is fixed, an element
g\in G(\mathbb A_{f}^{p})
defines a prime-to-
p
isogeny:

(A_{0},\iota_{0},\Lambda_{0},\bar{\eta}_{0})\to(A_{0},\iota_{0},\Lambda_{0},\overline{g^{-1}\circ\eta_{0}}).

Two elements differing by an element of
K^{p}
define the same isogeny. Conversely, any prime-to-
p
isogeny is induced by a well-defined element of
G(\mathbb A_{f}^{p})/K^{p}
.

An isogeny
\alpha:(A_{0},\iota_{0},\Lambda_{0})\to(A,\iota,\Lambda)
, whose degree is divisible by
p
, defines
(A,\iota,\Lambda,\bar{\eta})
, where
\bar{\eta}
is the composition

H^{1}(A,\mathbb A_{f}^{p})\overset{\alpha}{}H^{1}(A_{0},\mathbb A_{f}^{p})\xrightarrow{\eta_{0}}V\otimes\mathbb A_{f}^{p}.

We now consider the
p
-divisible group
X_{0}
of
A_{0}
and its
contravariant
Dieudonné module
M_{0}
([Dem]). The cotangent space of
X_{0}
canonically identifies with
M_{0}/FM_{0}
([Fo], III.4.3). Then
M_{0}
is equipped with
†
†
margin:

205

an action of
O_{D}\otimes\mathbb{Z}_{p}
and a class of perfect alternating bilinear forms
\psi
, which differ from one another by units in
O_{L_{0}}\otimes O_{\mathfrak{k}}
. Here
O_{\mathfrak{k}}
denotes the ring of Witt vectors of
\mathbb{F}
.
We extend the action of
D\otimes\mathbb{Q}_{p}
and the class of alternating bilinear forms to the rational Dieudonné module
M_{0\mathbb{Q}}
. Let
Y_{p}
denote the set of sublattices
M\subset M_{0\mathbb{Q}}
that satisfy the following conditions:

(i)

M
is invariant under the action of
O_{D}
,

(ii)

There exists an element
c(M)\in(L_{0}\otimes O_{\mathfrak{k}})^{\times}
such that the dual space with respect to one of the forms
\psi
is
M^{*}=c(M)\cdot M
,

(iii)

M
is
V
- and
F
-invariant, and it satisfies

\text{Tr}(x\mid M/FM)=t(x)

(equality of elements of
\mathbb{F}
).

Lemma 6.5
.

The
V
-invariance required in (iii) follows from the condition (ii) and the
F
-invariance.

Proof.

We have

\psi(Fu,Fv)=p\cdot\psi(u,v)^{\sigma},

where
\sigma:O_{\mathfrak{k}}\to O_{\mathfrak{k}}
denotes the Frobenius on the Witt vectors. From
FV=p
, it follows that

\psi(u,Vv)=\psi(Fu,v)^{\sigma^{-1}}.

Thus, we have

(FM)^{*}=V^{-1}M^{*}=V^{-1}\cdot c(M)M=c(M)^{\sigma^{-1}}\cdot V^{-1}M.

From the inclusion
FM\subset M
, it follows that
VM\subset M
. ∎

Let
\alpha:(A_{0},\iota_{0},\Lambda_{0},\bar{\eta}_{0})\rightarrow(A,\iota,\Lambda,\bar{\eta})
be an isogeny. The Dieudonné module of
A
becomes an element of
Y_{p}
via
\alpha
. By decomposing
\alpha
into its prime-to-
p
component and its
p
-primary component, we obtain an element of
(G(\mathbb{A}^{p}_{f})/K^{p})\times Y_{p}
. Its image in

Y(K)\underset{\text{Def}}{=}\text{Aut}(A_{0},\iota_{0},\Lambda_{0})\backslash\big(G(\mathbb{A}^{p}_{f})/K^{p}\times Y_{p}\big)

depends only on
(A,\iota,\Lambda,\bar{\eta})\in\mathscr{J}_{K}
and not on the choice of the isogeny. Indeed, if
\alpha
is a composition,

(A_{0},\iota_{0},\Lambda_{0},\bar{\eta}_{0})\xrightarrow{\alpha^{p}}(A^{\prime},\iota^{\prime},\Lambda^{\prime},\bar{\eta}^{\prime})\xrightarrow{\alpha_{p}}(A,\iota,\Lambda,\bar{\eta}),

with
\eta=\eta^{\prime}\circ\alpha_{p}^{*}
, we obtain
g
by writing
\eta^{\prime}=g^{-1}\circ\eta_{0}\circ\alpha^{p*}
. Then we regard
\alpha_{p}
as an isogeny from
A_{0}
to
A
to embed
M(A)
into
M(A_{0})
. Via
\eta_{0}
, we map
\text{Aut}(A_{0},\iota_{0},\Lambda_{0})
into
G(\mathbb{A}^{p}_{f})
, and if we replace
\alpha
by
\alpha\circ\gamma
,
\gamma\in\text{Aut}(A_{0},\iota_{0},\Lambda_{0})
,
†
†
margin:

206

then
g
is replaced by
\gamma^{-1}g
. Moreover,
M(A)
is replaced by
\gamma^{-1}M(A)
, where it is important to note that
\gamma^{-1}M(A)
depends only on the
p
-primary component of
\gamma
.

We first show that the mapping just defined is a bijection from
\mathscr{J}_{K}
to
Y(K)
. The theory of Dieudonné modules immediately shows that every element of
G(\mathbb{A}^{p}_{f})/K^{p}\times Y_{p}
defines an isogeny
\alpha
, so surjectivity is clear. Conversely, if
\alpha_{1}
and
\alpha_{2}
are two isogenies with the same image in
Y(K)
, we obtain two elements in
G(\mathbb{A}^{p}_{f})/K^{p}\times Y_{p}
that differ by an element
\alpha\in\text{Aut}(A_{0},\iota_{0},\Lambda_{0})
. Let
\gamma=\frac{1}{n}\cdot\gamma^{\prime}
, where
\gamma^{\prime}
is an endomorphism of the abelian variety
A_{0}
(and
n
is a power of
p
). If we replace
\alpha_{1}
by
\alpha_{1}\circ\gamma^{\prime}
and
\alpha_{2}
by
\alpha_{2}\circ n
, we may assume that the elements of
G(\mathbb{A}^{p}_{f})/K^{p}\times Y_{p}
defined by
\alpha_{1}
and
\alpha_{2}
are identical. Let

\varphi_{1}=\alpha_{2}\alpha_{1}^{-1},\quad\text{and}\quad\varphi_{2}=\alpha_{1}^{-1}\alpha_{2}.

If we can show that
\varphi_{1}
and
\varphi_{2}
are morphisms in the category of abelian varieties up to prime-to-
p
isogeny and not just isogenies, then they define mutually inverse
isomorphisms
between
(A_{1},\iota_{1},\Lambda_{1})
and
(A_{2},\iota_{2},\Lambda_{2})
, which are also compatible with the level structures. Let (with a new
n
)
\varphi_{1}=\frac{1}{n}\cdot\varphi_{1}^{\prime}
with
\varphi_{1}^{\prime}\in\text{Hom}(A_{1},A_{2})
. On the Dieudonné modules, we obtain an inclusion

\varphi_{1}^{\prime}(M_{2})\subset M_{1}.

But since, by assumption, the elements of
Y_{p}
defined by
\alpha_{1}
and
\alpha_{2}
are identical, we have
\varphi_{1}^{\prime}(M_{2})\subset n\cdot M_{1}
, and thus
\varphi_{1}^{\prime}
factors through the quotient of
A_{1}
by its
n
-torsion points, so
\varphi_{1}
is a morphism. Similarly, one shows that
\varphi_{2}
is a morphism. In the constructed bijection, the action of
\Phi
on
\mathscr{J}_{K}
corresponds to the operator on
Y(K)
that acts on the
Y_{p}
-component and replaces the lattice
M
by the lattice
F^{r}M
. This follows immediately from the fact that the relative Frobenius
\text{Fr}:A\rightarrow A^{(p)}
defines a map on the Dieudonné modules,
M^{(p)}\rightarrow M
, which identifies the lattice
M^{(p)}
with
FM
(see [Dem], p. 63).

Next, we want to compare the sets
Y(K)
and
X_{\phi}(K)
. After choosing a
D
-linear
L_{0}
-symplectic similarity
M_{0\mathbb{Q}}\cong V\otimes{\mathfrak{k}}
, the group
G({\mathfrak{k}})\rtimes\text{Gal}(\overline{\mathbb{Q}}_{p}/\mathbb{Q}_{p})
acts on
M_{0}
. (Here,
V
is again a
D
-module.) The set of lattices
M\subset M_{0\mathbb{Q}}
satisfying conditions (i) and (ii) before
6.5
forms a homogeneous space under
G({\mathfrak{k}})
and can be identified with
\mathscr{X}=G({\mathfrak{k}})\cdot x^{0}
(existence of a normal form). Due to the nature of the identification of
\mathscr{P}
with
\mathscr{M}
,
\xi_{p}=\phi\circ\zeta_{p}
determines the rational Dieudonné module
(M_{0\mathbb{Q}},F)
up to isomorphism. This means that there exists an element
h_{1}\in G({\mathfrak{k}})
such that

F=\text{ad}\,{h_{1}}(F_{\phi}),

where
F_{\phi}=\xi^{\prime}(d_{\sigma})
is the “Frobenius” constructed before (
5
). Here,
d_{\sigma}\in W_{L_{n}/\mathbb{Q}_{p}}
denotes a lift of the Frobenius element in
\text{Gal}(L_{n}/\mathbb{Q}_{p})
, and
\xi^{\prime}
denotes the homomorphism from the Weil group
†
†
margin:

207

W_{L_{n}/\mathbb{Q}_{p}}
to
G({\mathfrak{k}})\rtimes\text{Gal}({\mathfrak{k}}/\mathbb{Q}_{p})
used in the construction of
\xi_{p}^{\prime}=\text{ad}\,h(\xi_{p}):\mathscr{D}_{L_{n}}^{L_{n}}\rightarrow\mathscr{G}_{G}
. In the above equation, the right hand side is of course to be interpreted as the operator on
M_{0\mathbb{Q}}
defined by the element there. We show that the condition

\text{inv}(x^{1},x^{2})=\mu,

where
x^{i}
corresponds to the lattice
M^{i}
, is equivalent to the inclusion

pM^{1}\subset M^{2}\subset M^{1},

such that
\text{Tr}(x\mid M^{1}/M^{2})=t(x)
,
x\in\mathcal{O}_{D}
.

Clearly, it suffices to find two lattices
M^{1},M^{2}
satisfying the above condition, and such that for the corresponding points
x^{i}\in\mathscr{X}
, the invariant is equal to
\mu
. Let
T^{\prime}
be a Cartan subgroup of
G
defined over
\mathbb{Q}_{p}
that splits over
{\mathfrak{k}}
, and let
\mu^{\prime}\in X_{*}(T^{\prime})
be a cocharacter conjugate to
\mu
. We choose in the apartment corresponding to
T^{\prime}
the special point
x^{1}
corresponding to
T^{\prime}(\mathbb{Z}_{p})
([T], §3.6.1), which corresponds to a lattice
M^{1}
. Then
T^{\prime}(\mathcal{O}_{\mathfrak{k}})
acts on
M^{1}
and
T^{\prime}(\mathbb{F})
acts on
M^{1}/pM^{1}
, and the resulting representation is the reduction modulo
p
of the representation of
T^{\prime}
on
V_{\mathbb{Q}_{p}}
. We set

M^{2}=p\cdot M^{1}+\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\langle\mu^{\prime},\chi\rangle=0\end{subarray}}\mathbb{F}_{\chi}\subset M^{1},

where
\mathbb{F}_{\chi}
is the character space corresponding to
\chi
. Since
\mu^{\prime}
is conjugate to
\mu
, the characters appearing in
M^{1}/M^{2}
are precisely those characters
\chi
that appear in
V{C}
and for which
\langle\mu,\chi\rangle=1
. Thus,

\mathrm{Tr}(x\mid M^{1}/M^{2})=\text{Tr}(x\mid V^{1,0}_{h})\pmod{p}

for
x\in O_{D}
. It is also clear that
x^{2}=p^{\mu^{\prime}}x^{1}
, so

\text{inv}(x^{1},x^{2})=-\text{inv}(x^{2},x^{1})=\mu

(cf. example before
5.2
). We obtain that for
M\in Y(K)
,

\text{inv}(M,FM)=\mu.

It follows that
h_{1}:\mathscr{X}\rightarrow\mathscr{X}
establishes a bijection between
X_{p}
and
Y_{p}
, which transforms the operator
\Phi
into the action of Frobenius, and that
g\mapsto h_{1}\cdot g\cdot h_{1}^{-1}
defines an isomorphism between
J_{\phi}
and
\text{Aut}(M_{0\mathbb{Q}},\iota_{0},\Lambda_{0})
. From the definition of the motivic gerbe, it follows that we can identify
I_{\phi}
with
\text{Aut}(A_{0},\iota_{0},\Lambda_{0})
, as well as the action of
I_{\phi}
on
X^{p}/K^{p}\times X_{p}
with the action of
\text{Aut}(A_{0},\iota_{0},\Lambda_{0})
on
G(\mathbb{A}^{p}_{f})/K^{p}\times Y_{p}
, provided we choose the base point of
X^{p}/K^{p}
determined by the
l
-adic cohomology of
A_{0}
and identify
X_{p}
with
Y_{p}
and
J_{\phi}
with
\text{Aut}(M_{0\mathbb{Q}},\iota_{0},\Lambda_{0})
in the manner described above. We obtain the desired bijection between
X_{\phi}(K)
and
Y(K)
.

7.
Two examples

The
†
†
margin:

208

first example will be a
z
-extension in the sense of Kottwitz [K1],

1\to A\to G^{\prime}\to G\to 1,

such that the conjecture of §5 cannot hold simultaneously for both
G
and
G^{\prime}
. In this example, the derived groups of
G^{\prime}
and
G
cannot both be simply connected. Below we will construct a group
G^{\prime\prime}
over
\mathbb{Q}
and a map
h^{\prime\prime}:\mathbb S\to G^{\prime\prime}_{\mathbb{R}}
with the following properties:

(7.a)

(G^{\prime\prime},h^{\prime\prime})
defines a Shimura variety.

(7.b)
Let
Z^{\prime\prime}
be the center of
G^{\prime\prime}
. Then

H^{1}(\mathbb{Q},Z^{\prime\prime})=0\quad\text{and}\quad H^{1}(\mathbb{Q}_{v},Z^{\prime\prime})=0

for each prime
v
of
\mathbb{Q}
.

(7.c) Let
Z^{\prime\prime}_{\text{der}}
be the center of
G^{\prime\prime}_{\mathrm{der}}
. Then the map

H^{2}(\mathbb{Q},Z^{\prime\prime}_{\text{der}})\to\prod_{v}H^{2}(\mathbb{Q}_{v},Z^{\prime\prime}_{\text{der}})

is injective.

(7.d)
There exists a finite algebraic group
U
over
\mathbb{Q}
and an embedding
u^{\prime\prime}:U\to Z^{\prime\prime}_{\text{der}}
such that the map

H^{2}(\mathbb{Q},U)\to\prod_{v}H^{2}(\mathbb{Q}_{v},U)

is not injective.

We embed
U
in an induced torus
A

u:U\to A

and set

G^{\prime}=A\times G^{\prime\prime}/(u\times u^{\prime\prime})(U),\quad G=G^{\prime\prime}/u^{\prime\prime}(U).

Then
G^{\prime}
is a
z
-extension of
G
by
A
as above, and

G^{\prime}_{\mathrm{der}}=G^{\prime\prime}_{\mathrm{der}},\quad G_{\mathrm{der}}=G^{\prime\prime}_{\mathrm{der}}/u^{\prime\prime}(U).

Let
\bar{G}
be an inner twist of
G
, and
\bar{G}^{\prime}
and
\bar{G}^{\prime\prime}
the corresponding twistings of
G^{\prime}
and
G^{\prime\prime}
.

Next
†
†
margin:

209

, we define a cocycle
g_{\sigma}^{\prime}
with values in
\bar{G}^{\prime}
, whose image in
\bar{G}
is locally trivial everywhere and globally trivial in
G_{\text{ab}}
, but whose image in
G^{\prime}_{\text{ab}}
is not trivial. Since
A
is an induced torus, we obtain a commutative diagram with exact rows:

\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{H^{1}(\mathbb{Q},A/U)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{H^{2}(\mathbb{Q},U)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{H^{2}(\mathbb{Q},A)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\prod_{v}H^{1}(\mathbb{Q}_{v},A/U)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\prod_{v}H^{2}(\mathbb{Q}_{v},U)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\prod_{v}H^{2}(\mathbb{Q}_{v},A).}

The map in the last column is injective. Consequently, the class
\{a_{\varrho,\sigma}\}\in H^{2}(\mathbb{Q},U)
, which is locally trivial everywhere and whose existence we required in (
7
), is the image of a class in
H^{1}(\mathbb{Q},A/U)
. Let
\sigma\mapsto a^{\prime}_{\sigma}\in A
be a lift of a representative of this class. The coboundary of this cochain is a 2-cocycle with values in
Z^{\prime\prime}_{\text{der}}
, which is locally trivial everywhere and thus trivial, according to (
7
). Let
\{z_{\sigma}\}
be a cochain whose coboundary is the inverse of the coboundary of
\{a_{\sigma}^{\prime}\}
. Then,

\sigma\mapsto g^{\prime}_{\sigma}=a^{\prime}_{\sigma}\cdot z_{\sigma}

is a cocycle with values in
A\cdot Z^{\prime\prime}_{\text{der}}/U\subset\bar{G}^{\prime}
. Its image
g_{\sigma}
in
\bar{G}=\bar{G}^{\prime\prime}/U
is the image of
z_{\sigma}
. Since
G_{\text{ab}}=\bar{G}^{\prime\prime}/\bar{G}^{\prime\prime}_{\text{der}}
, its class in
G_{\text{ab}}
is obviously trivial. Locally,

z_{\sigma}=v_{\sigma}\cdot u_{\sigma},

where
v_{\sigma}
is a cocycle with values in
Z_{\text{der}}^{\prime\prime}
and
u_{\sigma}\in U
. Consequently,
g_{\sigma}
is also the image of
v_{\sigma}
and is thus trivial by (
7
). Therefore, the class
\{g_{\sigma}\}
is locally trivial everywhere in
\bar{G}
.

We define
h^{\prime}
as
1\times h^{\prime\prime}
, and define
h
as the composition of
h^{\prime}
and the projection from
G^{\prime}
to
G
. Then,
(G^{\prime},h^{\prime})
and
(G,h)
are Shimura varieties. The natural map
\mathscr{G}_{G^{\prime}}\to\mathscr{G}_{G}
assigns to each homomorphism
\phi^{\prime}:\mathscr{Q}\to\mathscr{G}_{G^{\prime}}
a homomorphism
\phi:\mathscr{Q}\to\mathscr{G}_{G}
.

Lemma 7.1
.

Two homomorphisms
\phi_{1}^{\prime}
and
\phi_{2}^{\prime}
, which on the kernel induce the same homomorphism to
G_{\text{ab}}^{\prime}
, and give rise to equivalent homomorphisms
\phi_{1}
and
\phi_{2}
, are equivalent.

The homomorphisms
\phi_{1}
and
\phi_{2}
are equal. The following sequence is exact:

1\to U\to G^{\prime}\to G_{\text{ab}}^{\prime}\times G.

From the assumptions, it follows easily that
\phi_{1}
and
\phi_{2}
induce identical homomorphisms on the kernel. The statement then follows from
H^{1}(\mathbb{Q},A)=0
.

We
†
†
margin:

210

have already seen (Remark before
5.3
) that
\text{Sh}_{K^{\prime}}(G^{\prime},h^{\prime})
is a surjective covering space of
\text{Sh}_{K}(G,h)
with the fiber

A(\mathbb{Q})\backslash\alpha^{-1}(K)/K^{\prime}.

Next, we want to show that if
\phi^{\prime}
and
\phi
are admissible, where
\phi
is induced by
\phi^{\prime}
, the set

Y_{\phi^{\prime}}=I_{\phi^{\prime}}\backslash X_{p}^{\prime}\times X^{\prime p}/K^{\prime p}

is a surjective covering of the corresponding set
X_{\phi}
with the same fiber. Thus, the conjecture of §5 can only hold for
G^{\prime}
and
G
simultaneously, at least in a compatible way, if every admissible homomorphism
\phi
comes from an admissible homomorphism
\phi^{\prime}
. We will later find an admissible
\phi^{\prime}
such that
\phi^{\prime}
is rational on the kernel and
I=G^{\prime}
. Then,
\mathscr{I}_{\phi^{\prime}}=\bar{G}^{\prime}
and
\mathscr{I}_{\phi}=\bar{G}
are inner twistings of
G^{\prime}
and
G
. Let
\{g_{\sigma}^{\prime}\}
be the cocycle constructed above and
\{g_{\sigma}\}
its image in
\bar{G}
. Then

g\cdot\phi:\mathscr{Q}\to\mathscr{G}_{G}

is admissible. In fact, because the image of
\{g_{\sigma}\}
in
G_{\text{ab}}
is trivial, the validity of condition (
5
) is preserved under the transition from
\phi
to
g\cdot\phi
, and since
\{g_{\sigma}\}
is locally trivial everywhere, conditions (
5
), (
5
), (
5
) are also preserved. The homomorphism
g^{\prime}\cdot\phi^{\prime}
gives rise to
g\cdot\phi
and is not admissible because the image of
g_{\sigma}
in
G^{\prime}_{\text{ab}}
is not trivial. If there were an admissible homomorphism
\widetilde{\phi}^{\prime}
that gives
\phi
, it would induce the same homomorphism on the kernel as
g^{\prime}\cdot\phi^{\prime}
, and thus, by Lemma
7.1
, would coincide with
g^{\prime}\cdot\phi^{\prime}
, which is impossible.

To prove the above statement, we specify that we take
G^{\prime}
and
G
to be quasi-split over
\mathbb{Q}_{p}
, and that
K_{p}^{\prime}
and
K_{p}
are chosen to be hyperspecial. The torus
A
splits over an unramified extension. We may assume that the restriction of
\phi^{\prime}
to the kernel is defined over
\mathbb{Q}
. Then,
I_{\phi^{\prime}}=\mathscr{I}_{\phi^{\prime}}(\mathbb{Q})
and
I_{\phi}=\mathscr{I}_{\phi}(\mathbb{Q})
, and the sequence

1\to A\to\mathscr{I}_{\phi^{\prime}}\to\mathscr{I}_{\phi}\to 1

is exact. Hence,

I_{\phi^{\prime}}\to I_{\phi}

is surjective. Similarly, one concludes that
X^{\prime p}\to X^{p}
is surjective. Since
G^{\prime}({\mathfrak{k}})\to G({\mathfrak{k}})
is also surjective,
\mathscr{X}^{\prime}\to\mathscr{X}
is surjective. Here,

\mathscr{X}^{\prime}\subset\mathscr{B}(G^{\prime},{\mathfrak{k}})=\mathscr{B}(A,{\mathfrak{k}})\times\mathscr{B}(G^{\prime\prime},{\mathfrak{k}}).

Let
x^{\prime}=(g^{\prime}y^{0},g^{\prime}x^{0})\in\mathscr{X}^{\prime}
with image in
X_{p}
:

\text{inv}((gx^{0}),F(gx^{0}))=\mu^{\prime\prime}=\mu.

Then
†
†
margin:

211

we have

\displaystyle\text{inv}\big((g^{\prime}y^{0},g^{\prime}x^{0}),F(g^{\prime}y^{0},g^{\prime}x^{0})\big)

\displaystyle=\text{inv}\big((g^{\prime}y^{0},g^{\prime}x^{0}),(\sigma(g^{\prime})y^{0},Fg^{\prime}x^{0})\big)

\displaystyle=0+\mu^{\prime\prime},

if
g^{\prime}y^{0}
is rational over
\mathbb{Q}_{p}
. Since we can always find such a
g^{\prime}
,
X_{p}^{\prime}\to X_{p}
is surjective.
Now consider the map

X_{p}^{\prime}\times X^{\prime p}\to X_{p}\times X^{p}.

If
x^{\prime}_{p}\times x^{\prime p}
and
\bar{x}^{\prime}_{p}\times\bar{x}^{\prime p}
have the same image in
Y_{\phi}
, then for the images in
X_{p}\times X^{p}
,

\bar{x}_{p}\times\bar{x}^{p}=\gamma(x_{p}\times x^{p})k^{p},\quad\gamma\in I_{\phi},\quad k^{p}\in K^{p}.

Since
I_{\phi^{\prime}}\to I_{\phi}
is surjective, we can, after changing
\bar{x}_{p}^{\prime}\times\bar{x}^{\prime p}
by
\gamma^{\prime}
, assume that
\gamma=1
. Then,

\displaystyle\bar{x}^{\prime}_{p}

\displaystyle=a\cdot x^{\prime}_{p}\quad a\in A(\mathbb{Q}_{p}),

\displaystyle\bar{x}^{\prime p}

\displaystyle=x^{\prime p}g^{\prime}\quad g^{\prime}\in\alpha^{-1}(K^{p}).

Thus,
Y_{\phi^{\prime}}\to Y_{\phi}
is a surjective covering with fiber

A(\mathbb{Q})\backslash\alpha^{-1}(K^{p})\cdot A(\mathbb{Q}_{p})/K^{\prime p}\cdot V,

where
V
is the stabilizer of
y^{0}
in
A(\mathbb{Q}_{p})
. The statement follows because
K_{p}^{\prime}\to K_{p}
is surjective, and

\alpha^{-1}(K_{p})/K_{p}^{\prime}\cong A(\mathbb{Q}_{p})/V.

It remains to construct an example. Let
L
be a totally imaginary quadratic extension of a totally real number field
L_{0}
. Let

be a 4-dimensional Hermitian form with coefficients in

, and let

be the corresponding group of unitary similarities:

H_{J}(\mathbb{Q})=\{A\in\text{GL}(4,L)\mid A^{*}JA=\lambda\cdot J\}.

The center of

is
L^{\times}
. The group
G^{\prime\prime}
will be a product of the form

G^{\prime\prime}=\prod_{(L^{i},L_{0}^{i},J^{i})}H_{J^{i}},

and
h^{\prime\prime}
is chosen as usual so that (

7
) is obvious. To check (

. Then
Z_{\text{der}}^{\prime\prime}
is the induced module

Z_{\text{der}}^{\prime\prime}=\text{Ind}^{\text{Gal}(\overline{\mathbb{Q}}/\mathbb{Q})}_{\text{Gal}(\overline{\mathbb{Q}}/L_{0})}\mathbb{Z}/4.

It
†
†
margin:

must be shown that

is injective, or equivalently, by the Poitou-Tate theorem ([Se1], p. II-49), that

H^{1}(L_{0},\mu_{4})\to\prod_{v}H^{1}(L_{0v},\mu_{4})

is injective. This follows from the Grunewald-Wang theorem ([AT], Chap. 10, Th. 1), because
-1\in L_{0}
is not a square.

Suppose we can find a totally real Galois extension
L_{0}
of
\mathbb{Q}
and a Galois module

over

We require that

, that

-module for a Galois CM-extension

\mathbb{Q}

\displaystyle L

\displaystyle=\mathbb{Q}(\sqrt{-1},\sqrt{73},\sqrt{89})=\mathbb{Q}(\sqrt{-1})\cdot\mathbb{Q}(\sqrt{73},\sqrt{89})

\displaystyle=K\cdot L_{0},

with
K=\mathbb{Q}(\sqrt{-1})
. Then,

\displaystyle\text{Gal}(L/\mathbb{Q})

\displaystyle=\text{Gal}(L/L_{0})\times\text{Gal}(L/K)

\displaystyle\cong\mathbb{Z}/2\times(\mathbb{Z}/2\oplus\mathbb{Z}/2).

It is easy to verify that all decomposition groups are either trivial or cyclic of order 2. This is clear at the archimedean places and the places outside 2, 73, and 89, because at those places, the extension
L
is unramified. In
\mathbb{Q}_{2}
, 73 and 89 are squares because
73\equiv 89\equiv 1\mod 8
. Accordingly,

\left(\frac{-1}{73}\right)=1,\quad\left(\frac{89}{73}\right)=\left(\frac{16}{73}\right)=1

and

\left(\frac{-1}{89}\right)=1,\quad\left(\frac{73}{89}\right)=\left(\frac{-16}{89}\right)=1.

Let

(i)

So on the left side of this equation is the usual action of

We must now compare this 2-cocycle of

Thus, we see that the two cocycles being compared are locally equivalent everywhere and hence equivalent. After correcting

We still need to prove that

,

is equivalent to the canonical neutralization of

.

The
†
†
margin:

composition
\phi\circ\zeta_{\infty}
is, on the kernel
\mathbb{C}^{\times}
of

, equal to the diagonal homomorphism
\mathbb{C}^{i}\to G(\mathbb{C})=\prod{\rm GL}(2,\mathbb{C})
. The corresponding cohomology class in

was determined earlier. Since

determines the same cohomology class. Since the center of

, it follows that

are equivalent.

The condition at
p
is more complicated to verify. The building of
G
is a product

\mathscr{B}(G,{\mathfrak{k}})=\mathscr{B}(G_{\text{der}},{\mathfrak{k}})\times X_{*}(G_{\text{ab}})\otimes\mathbb{R},

and it is the same as the building of
R_{E_{p}/\mathbb{Q}_{p}}{\rm GL}(2)
, albeit with the twisted action. On
X_{*}(G_{\text{ab}})\otimes\mathbb{R}
, this action agrees with the usual one, while on the building
\mathscr{B}(R_{E_{p}/\mathbb{Q}_{p}}\mathrm{SL}(2),{\mathfrak{k}})
, the twisted action of
\sigma
is given by

\sigma(x^{0},\dots,x^{n-1})=(x^{1},\dots,x^{n-1},j\cdot\sigma^{n}_{*}(x^{0})).

Here the
x^{i}
are in the building of
\mathrm{SL}(2)
. Let

x_{0}=(x_{0}^{0},\dots,x^{n-1}_{0})\in\mathscr{B}(G,{\mathfrak{k}})

be the vertex corresponding to the maximal compact subgroup of the integral elements of
{\rm GL}(2,E)
. Then it is clear that
\sigma^{2n}(x_{0})=x_{0}
, and if we denote the translates of
x_{0}
under the Galois group by
x_{i}=\sigma^{i}(x_{0})
, the
x_{i}
for
i\in\mathbb{Z}/2n
form the vertices of a polysimplex that is invariant under
\text{Gal}({\mathfrak{k}}/\mathbb{Q}_{p})
. We consider its factor in
\mathscr{B}(G_{\text{der}},{\mathfrak{k}})
. It is easy to see that its center of mass is the unique fixed point of the Galois action on the building of
G_{\text{der}}
. By the Fixed Point Theorem of Bruhat and Tits, the building
\mathscr{B}(G_{\text{der}},\mathbb{Q}_{p})
consists of this single point. Since we had chosen
K_{p}
to be the well-defined maximal compact subgroup of
G(\mathbb{Q}_{p})
, i.e.,
K_{p}\cap G_{\text{der}}(\mathbb{Q}_{p})=G_{\text{der}}(\mathbb{Q}_{p})
, and because the stabilizer of a chamber in a simply connected group fixes all points in the chamber, we have

K_{p}=\{g\in G(\mathbb{Q}_{p})\mid g\cdot x_{i}=x_{i},\,i\in\mathbb{Z}/2n\}.

Thus, we have represented
K_{p}
in the form used in §5 for the formulation of the admissibility conditions. Next, we calculate the operator
F
. Let
v_{1}=2a+1
and
v_{2}=2b+1
, so
a+b=n-1
. We choose the fundamental class for the unramified extension
L_{2n}=L_{{\mathfrak{p}}_{1}}
of degree
2n
as in the example before
5.2
. We send the element
d_{\sigma}=1\times\sigma\in\mathscr{D}_{L_{2n}}^{L_{2n}}
to

F=\left(\begin{pmatrix}p\\
&1\end{pmatrix},\dots,\begin{pmatrix}1\\
&p\end{pmatrix},\dots,\begin{pmatrix}1\\
&p\end{pmatrix},j\right)\times\sigma.

Here
†
†
margin:

219

the matrix
\begin{pmatrix}p\\
&1\end{pmatrix}
occurs
a
times and
\begin{pmatrix}1\\
&p\end{pmatrix}
occurs
b
times. On the kernel, we define the homomorphism

\nu:\lambda\mapsto\lambda_{1}v_{1}+\lambda_{2}v_{2}.

To verify that a homomorphism
\widetilde{\xi}:\mathscr{D}^{L_{2n}}\to\mathscr{G}_{G}
is defined in this way, it suffices to show that
F^{2n}=\nu(p^{-1})
in
G(\mathbb{Q}_{p})=G^{1}(E_{p})
. By using the fact that both
\sigma^{n}
and the element
j
act by swapping the entries of a diagonal matrix,

\sigma^{n}\begin{pmatrix}s_{1}\\
&s_{2}\end{pmatrix}=\begin{pmatrix}s_{2}\\
&s_{1}\end{pmatrix}=j\begin{pmatrix}s_{1}\\
&s_{2}\end{pmatrix}j^{-1},

we obtain

F^{n}=\begin{pmatrix}p^{a}\\
&p^{b}\end{pmatrix}j\times\sigma^{n}\in G^{1}(E_{p})\times\sigma^{n}.

Hence we have

F^{2n}=\begin{pmatrix}p^{2a+1}\\
&p^{2b+1}\end{pmatrix}=\begin{pmatrix}p^{v_{1}}\\
&p^{v_{2}}\end{pmatrix}=\nu(p^{-1}).

The localized homomorphism
\phi\circ\zeta_{p}
agrees with
\widetilde{\xi}
on the kernel. Therefore, they differ by a 1-cocycle with values in
T
, and since
H^{1}(\mathbb{Q}_{p},T)=0
, they are equivalent. This implies that the image of
d_{\sigma}
is the operator
F
defined in §5. Clearly we have

\displaystyle{\rm inv}(\sigma x_{i},Fx_{i})

\displaystyle={\rm inv}\left(x_{i+1},\left(\begin{pmatrix}p\\
&1\end{pmatrix},\dots,\begin{pmatrix}1\\
&p\end{pmatrix},j\right)\cdot x_{i+1}\right)

\displaystyle=(\mu,\dots,\mu).

Hence
X_{p}
contains the point
(x_{0},\dots,x_{2n-1})
and is non-empty. Therefore,
\phi
also satisfies the admissibility condition (
5
).
